================================================================================
  STAGE 13: PhD RESEARCH YEARS — QUESTIONS AND ANSWERS
  Frontier topics and research skills
================================================================================

How to use this file (note for the parent / teacher)
----------------------------------------------------
- There are 32 topics (13.1 to 13.32, the same as in topic.txt), in two
  groups: the Advanced research-level topics (13.1-13.22: algebraic and
  arithmetic geometry, automorphic forms, advanced algebraic and geometric
  topology, quantum topology, mirror symmetry, advanced differential
  geometry, index theory, operator algebras, nonlinear PDE, dynamical
  systems, advanced probability, optimal transport, topological data
  analysis, higher categories, additive combinatorics, geometric group
  theory, mathematics of machine learning, formal verification, quantum
  information and the great open problems) and the Research skills
  (13.23-13.32: reading papers, literature search, choosing a problem,
  examples and counterexamples, writing, presenting, collaboration and
  ethics, teaching, the thesis, and life after the PhD).
  Each topic has:
    * WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN   the checklist of definitions, theorems,
                                  standard examples, counterexamples and
                                  methods in that topic
    * PART A — DESCRIPTIVE        12 questions (D1-D12): proofs,
                                  explanations, derivations and worked
                                  numerical problems, each with the answer
                                  and "Teacher explains" (the method step
                                  by step as a teacher shows it on the
                                  board, common mistakes, and a "Now you
                                  try" question)
    * PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY  12 questions (M1-M12): pure drills with
                                  answers. Most have many parts (a, b,
                                  c ...) so that EVERY case of the topic is
                                  practised again and again, from the
                                  easiest to the hardest — not just one
                                  example of each idea.
    * TEACHING TIPS               what to do if the student struggles, and
                                  how to make it harder
- In total: 384 descriptive + 384 mathematical questions.
- Level: PhD research. The expectation (topic.txt) is DEPTH IN ONE OR TWO
  of 13.1-13.22 and working AWARENESS of the others: a student should be
  able to do every "Part B" drill in every topic, and every "Part A"
  question in the topics of their own research area.
- The research-skill topics (13.23-13.32) are also practised with
  mathematics: every question there is attached to real computations,
  proofs, estimates or small research tasks, so the skills are learned by
  doing mathematics, not by reading advice.
- What is new in Stage 13: FROM LEARNING TO CREATING. Stage 12 taught the
  student to solve qualifying-exam problems; Stage 13 teaches the student
  to compute invariants in the modern theories, to test conjectures on
  examples, to find counterexamples, and to write and defend new results.
- Conventions: as in A12.txt. N = {1, 2, 3, ...}; log = ln unless a base is
  written; ≅ means isomorphic; □ ends a proof; exact answers first,
  decimals with ≈.
- Method: for every theorem know its hypotheses, the simplest example
  where it applies and the simplest example where it fails. Check every
  computation with software (SageMath, Macaulay2, Magma/PARI, GAP, Lean,
  Python) and write every solution in LaTeX.
- Do 3-4 hours a day: 1-2 descriptive questions (full written proof or
  essay) and 2-3 drills, plus one research paper per week read with the
  method of 13.23.
- Revise Stage 12 (A12.txt) whenever the student finds something
  difficult: 12.14-12.16 before 13.1-13.3; 12.9-12.10 before 13.4-13.9;
  12.2, 12.12-12.13 before 13.10-13.14; 12.21-12.23 before 13.16-13.18;
  12.26 before 13.19; 12.21 before 13.20; 12.27 before 13.21.

================================================================================
TOPIC 13.1 — ALGEBRAIC GEOMETRY: GROTHENDIECK'S THEORY (SCHEMES, SHEAVES,
MORPHISMS, COHOMOLOGY OF COHERENT SHEAVES), ÉTALE COHOMOLOGY, MODULI SPACES,
INTERSECTION THEORY, DERIVED ALGEBRAIC GEOMETRY
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - SPEC: for a ring A, Spec A = {prime ideals}, closed sets V(I), basic
    opens D(f); structure sheaf with O(D(f)) = A_f and stalk O_p = A_p.
    Closed points ↔ maximal ideals; every irreducible closed set has a
    unique GENERIC POINT. Spec A is quasi-compact. Examples: Spec ℤ (a
    generic point (0) plus one closed point per prime), Spec ℤ[x] (dim 2),
    Spec k[x]/(x²) (one point, non-reduced), Spec(ℤ/12) (two points).
  - SCHEMES: locally ringed spaces locally isomorphic to affine schemes.
    Hom(X, Spec A) = Hom(A, O_X(X)): affine schemes are anti-equivalent to
    rings. Gluing gives Pⁿ, Proj S, the line with doubled origin. Fibre
    products exist; FIBRE X_y = X ×_Y Spec κ(y). Properties: reduced,
    integral, noetherian, dimension, normal, REGULAR (dim m/m² = dim O).
    JACOBIAN CRITERION: V(f₁, ..., f_r) ⊂ Aⁿ_k of pure dim n − c is smooth
    at x iff rank(∂fᵢ/∂xⱼ)(x) = c.
  - SHEAVES: presheaves, gluing and locality axioms, stalks, SHEAFIFICATION
    (the universal sheaf), exactness is checked on stalks, so a surjection
    of sheaves need not be surjective on sections (exp: O → O* on ℂ*).
    O_X-modules; QUASI-COHERENT = locally M̃; COHERENT = locally M̃ with M
    finitely generated (X noetherian). On Spec A: qcoh ↔ A-modules.
  - LINE BUNDLES AND DIVISORS: Pic X = H¹(X, O*), Pic Pⁿ = ℤ·O(1), Cartier
    ↔ Weil on locally factorial X, Cl(Aⁿ) = 0, Cl of the cone xy = z² is
    ℤ/2, Pic(Spec O_K) = class group (ℤ[√−5]: ℤ/2).
  - MORPHISMS: finite type, affine, FINITE, closed immersion; SEPARATED
    (diagonal closed), PROPER (separated, finite type, universally
    closed). VALUATIVE CRITERIA (R a DVR, K = Frac R): maps Spec K → X
    extend to Spec R at most once (separated) / exactly once (proper).
    Projective ⇒ proper, finite ⇒ proper; A¹ → Spec k is not proper; the
    doubled-origin line is not separated.
  - FLAT, UNRAMIFIED, SMOOTH, ÉTALE: flat = ⊗ exact; over a Dedekind
    domain flat ⇔ torsion-free. For X ⊆ Pⁿ_Y closed, Y integral
    noetherian: X flat over Y ⇔ all fibres have the same HILBERT
    POLYNOMIAL. Unramified: Ω_{X/Y} = 0. ÉTALE = flat + unramified =
    smooth of relative dimension 0; SMOOTH = flat with geometrically
    regular fibres. A → A[T]/(g), g monic, is étale where g′(T) is a
    unit.
  - SHEAF COHOMOLOGY: Hⁱ(X, F) = right derived functors of Γ; long exact
    sequences; ČECH cohomology of an affine open cover computes it for
    qcoh F on separated X. SERRE: X affine ⇔ Hⁱ(X, F) = 0 for all qcoh F,
    i > 0 (X noetherian). GROTHENDIECK VANISHING: Hⁱ = 0 for i > dim X.
  - COHOMOLOGY OF Pⁿ (SERRE): H⁰(Pⁿ, O(d)) = forms of degree d, of
    dimension C(n + d, n); Hⁿ(Pⁿ, O(d)) ≅ H⁰(Pⁿ, O(−d − n − 1))^∨; all
    other Hⁱ vanish. Finite-dimensionality for coherent sheaves on
    projective X; SERRE VANISHING Hⁱ(F(m)) = 0, i > 0, m ≫ 0.
  - SERRE DUALITY: X smooth projective of dim n, Hⁱ(X, F) ≅ Hⁿ⁻ⁱ(X, F^∨ ⊗
    ω_X)^∨ for locally free F; ω_{Pⁿ} = O(−n − 1); ADJUNCTION ω_X =
    O_X(d − n − 1) for a smooth hypersurface of degree d in Pⁿ, and
    ω_X = O_X(∑dᵢ − n − 1) for complete intersections.
  - EULER CHARACTERISTIC χ(F) = ∑(−1)ⁱhⁱ(F); HILBERT POLYNOMIAL P_X(t) =
    χ(O_X(t)), of degree dim X with leading coefficient deg X/(dim X)!.
    Pⁿ: C(t + n, n); plane curve of degree d: dt + 1 − (d − 1)(d − 2)/2;
    twisted cubic 3t + 1; two skew lines 2t + 2; two meeting lines 2t + 1.
  - CURVES: RIEMANN-ROCH h⁰(D) − h⁰(K − D) = deg D + 1 − g, deg K = 2g − 2;
    RIEMANN-HURWITZ 2g_C − 2 = d(2g_D − 2) + ∑(e_P − 1) (tame);
    smooth plane curve g = (d − 1)(d − 2)/2; with δ nodes subtract δ;
    complete intersection (a, b) in P³: 2g − 2 = ab(a + b − 4).
  - SURFACES: intersection pairing on Pic S; ADJUNCTION 2g − 2 = C² + K·C;
    RIEMANN-ROCH χ(D) = χ(O_S) + (D² − K·D)/2; NOETHER χ(O_S) = (K² +
    c₂)/12; BLOW-UP at a point: E² = −1, K = π*K + E, K² drops by 1. On
    P¹ × P¹: (a, b)·(c, d) = ad + bc, K = (−2, −2), g(a, b) = (a−1)(b−1).
  - ÉTALE COHOMOLOGY: the Zariski topology is too coarse (a constant sheaf
    on an irreducible space is flasque, so Hⁱ = 0 for i > 0). ÉTALE SITE:
    covers by étale maps; H⁰ and H¹ via torsors; H^i(Spec k, F) = GALOIS
    COHOMOLOGY of Gal(k^sep/k). KUMMER 0 → μ_n → G_m → G_m → 0 (n
    invertible) and HILBERT 90 H¹_ét(X, G_m) = Pic X; ARTIN-SCHREIER 0 →
    ℤ/p → G_a → G_a → 0 in char p. For a smooth projective curve of genus
    g over k = k̄, ℓ ≠ char k: H⁰ = ℤ/ℓ, H¹ ≅ (ℤ/ℓ)^{2g}, H² ≅ ℤ/ℓ.
    COMPARISON (Artin): over ℂ, Hⁱ_ét(X, ℤ/n) ≅ Hⁱ(X(ℂ), ℤ/n). Proper and
    smooth base change, Poincaré duality, cd ≤ 2 dim X over k̄; ℓ-adic
    cohomology Hⁱ(X, ℚ_ℓ) = (lim Hⁱ(X, ℤ/ℓᵐ)) ⊗ ℚ_ℓ. π₁^ét(Spec F_q) = ℤ̂.
  - WEIL CONJECTURES (Grothendieck, Deligne): for X smooth projective over
    F_q, #X(F_{qᵐ}) = ∑(−1)ⁱ Tr(Fᵐ | Hⁱ) (LEFSCHETZ TRACE FORMULA), Z(X, t)
    is rational, satisfies a functional equation, and eigenvalues on Hⁱ
    have |α| = q^{i/2}. Elliptic curve: #E(F_q) = q + 1 − a, |a| ≤ 2√q,
    Z = (1 − at + qt²)/((1 − t)(1 − qt)).
  - MODULI: the FUNCTOR OF POINTS h_X = Hom(−, X); a FINE moduli space
    represents a moduli functor. Grassmannian G(k, n) (dim k(n − k),
    tangent Hom(V, kⁿ/V)); HILBERT SCHEME Hilb^P(Pⁿ) (projective,
    Grothendieck), tangent space Hom(I_Z, O_Z) = H⁰(N_Z) for Z lci,
    obstructions in H¹(N_Z); Hilbⁿ(surface) smooth of dim 2n (Fogarty);
    Picard scheme, Pic⁰ = Jacobian of dimension g.
  - COARSE MODULI AND STACKS: automorphisms prevent fine moduli (isotrivial
    non-trivial families such as quadratic twists); M_{1,1} has coarse
    space A¹ via j = 1728·4a³/(4a³ + 27b²) for y² = x³ + ax + b. Deformations
    of a curve: first order H¹(C, T_C), obstructions H²(C, T_C) = 0, so
    dim M_g = 3g − 3 (g ≥ 2); dim M_{g,n} = 3g − 3 + n; DELIGNE-MUMFORD
    compactification M̄_{g,n} by stable nodal curves; M̄_{0,4} ≅ P¹; A_g
    has dim g(g + 1)/2; GIT quotients (e.g. cubic surfaces: 19 − 15 = 4).
  - INTERSECTION THEORY (Fulton): Chow groups A_k(X) of cycles modulo
    rational equivalence; proper pushforward, flat pullback; A*(Pⁿ) =
    ℤ[H]/(H^{n+1}); BÉZOUT (degrees multiply for proper intersections);
    CHERN CLASSES (splitting principle), c(T_{Pⁿ}) = (1 + H)^{n+1};
    GAUSS-BONNET ∫c_top(T_X) = χ_top(X); the top Chern class of a vector
    bundle of rank = dim X counts zeros of a general section.
  - SCHUBERT CALCULUS: A*(G(2, 4)) generated by σ₁, σ₂, σ₁₁ with σ₁² = σ₂ +
    σ₁₁, σ₂² = σ₁₁² = 1, σ₂σ₁₁ = 0, σ₁⁴ = 2 (TWO lines meet 4 general
    lines). deg G(2, n) in Plücker = Catalan number C_{n−2}. 27 LINES on
    a smooth cubic surface = ∫c₄(Sym³S^∨) on G(2, 4); 2875 lines on a
    general quintic threefold = ∫c₆(Sym⁵S^∨) on G(2, 5).
  - LOCAL INTERSECTION MULTIPLICITY of plane curves I_P(f, g) = dim_k
    O_P/(f, g) ≥ m_P(f)m_P(g), with equality iff no common tangent line.
    SERRE'S TOR FORMULA: i(X·Y; Z) = ∑(−1)ⁱ length Torᵢ^{O}(O_X, O_Y) —
    needed when X or Y is not Cohen-Macaulay (two planes in A⁴ meeting a
    third plane: length of O_X ⊗ O_Y is 3, true multiplicity 2).
  - DERIVED ALGEBRAIC GEOMETRY: the derived category D^b(Coh X), derived
    functors Rf_*, Lf*, ⊗^L, RHom, Extⁱ, Serre functor −⊗ω_X[n],
    BEILINSON: D^b(P¹) = ⟨O, O(1)⟩, χ(O(a), O(b)) = b − a + 1 on P¹.
    DERIVED FIBRE PRODUCTS X ×^h_Z Y (Spec of A ⊗^L_C B, a simplicial or
    dg ring with π_i = Tor_i) and DERIVED ZERO LOCI of sections s of a
    rank r bundle on smooth M, of VIRTUAL DIMENSION dim M − r, always
    "correct" even when the actual intersection is not; their virtual
    classes give Serre's formula, c_top counts, and Gromov-Witten
    invariants (vdim of M̄_{0,n}(P², d) is 3d − 1 + n). Cotangent complex
    L_X = [I/I² → Ω|_X] for X = V(I) lci; the self-intersection of a
    point in A¹ is Spec k[ε], ε in homological degree 1, vdim −1.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Prove that Spec A is quasi-compact and that the D(f) form a basis
    of the Zariski topology. (b) Describe the morphism Spec ℤ[i] → Spec ℤ:
    find the fibres over (0), (2), (3), (5), their number of points,
    residue fields, and whether they are reduced. (c) Give a maximal chain
    of primes in ℤ[x] and state dim ℤ[x].
    Answer: (b) over (0): one point, κ = ℚ(i); over (2): one point (1 + i),
    fibre F₂[ε]/(ε²) non-reduced (RAMIFIED); over (3): one point, κ = F₉
    (INERT); over (5): two points (2 + i), (2 − i), κ = F₅ (SPLIT).
    (c) (0) ⊂ (5) ⊂ (5, x); dim ℤ[x] = 2.
    Teacher explains: (a) D(f) ∩ D(g) = D(fg), and an open set U = X ∖
    V(I) = ⋃_{f∈I} D(f), so the D(f) form a basis. If X = ⋃ D(fᵢ) then
    V(∑(fᵢ)) = ⋂V(fᵢ) = ∅, so the ideal (fᵢ) lies in no prime: (fᵢ) = A.
    Then 1 = a₁f_{i₁} + ... + a_m f_{i_m} is a FINITE sum, and the finitely
    many D(f_{iⱼ}) already cover. (b) The fibre over p is
       Spec(ℤ[i] ⊗ F_p) = Spec F_p[x]/(x² + 1),
    so everything is decided by factoring x² + 1 mod p.
       p = 2: x² + 1 = (x + 1)² in F₂[x]; fibre F₂[x]/((x + 1)²), ONE point
              with a nilpotent: the prime (1 + i) is ramified, 2 = −i(1+i)².
       p = 3: −1 is not a square mod 3 (squares: 0, 1), x² + 1 is
              irreducible, fibre Spec F₉: one point, INERT.
       p = 5: 2² = 4 ≡ −1, so x² + 1 = (x − 2)(x + 2); by CRT the fibre is
              Spec(F₅ × F₅), two points: (5, i − 2) = (2 − i) and
              (5, i + 2) = (2 + i), since 5 = (2 + i)(2 − i).
    The generic fibre is Spec(ℤ[i] ⊗ ℚ) = Spec ℚ(i), the generic point.
    In general p ≡ 1 (mod 4) splits and p ≡ 3 (mod 4) is inert. (c) (0)
    is prime (domain), ℤ[x]/(5) = F₅[x] is a domain, ℤ[x]/(5, x) = F₅ is
    a field. dim ℤ[x] = dim ℤ + 1 = 2 (ℤ noetherian). Points of Spec ℤ[x]:
    (0); (p); (f) with f irreducible of positive degree; (p, f) maximal
    with f irreducible mod p — Mumford's "arithmetic surface".
    Common mistakes: calling the fibre over 2 "one ordinary point" (the
    scheme remembers the nilpotent); forgetting the generic point;
    thinking (5) is maximal in ℤ[x].
    Now you try: the fibre of Spec ℤ[√−5] over 7. (x² + 5 ≡ x² − 2 and
    3² = 9 ≡ 2 mod 7: two points (7, √−5 − 3), (7, √−5 + 3), κ = F₇)

D2. (a) Show that a morphism of sheaves is surjective iff it is surjective
    on all stalks, and give an example where it is not surjective on
    global sections. (b) For X = ℂ* (analytic topology) use 0 → 2πiℤ → O
    → O* → 0 to compute the obstruction for z ∈ O*(X) to have a
    logarithm. (c) On G_m = Spec ℂ[x, x⁻¹] show that squaring O* → O* is
    NOT surjective as a map of Zariski sheaves but IS surjective as étale
    sheaves.
    Answer: (a) surjectivity of sheaves is defined stalkwise; exp on ℂ*.
    (b) the connecting map O*(X) → H¹(X, 2πiℤ) ≅ ℤ sends z ↦ 1 (winding
    number), so z has no logarithm. (c) x is not a square in ℂ(x), the
    stalk at the generic point; the étale cover x ↦ x² makes it one.
    Teacher explains: (a) The image presheaf of φ: F → G must be
    SHEAFIFIED; φ is surjective when im φ has sheafification G, which
    happens iff every germ of G is locally a φ-image, i.e. φ_x is onto for
    all x. A section s ∈ G(X) then lifts only LOCALLY: s|Uᵢ = φ(tᵢ); on
    Uᵢ ∩ Uⱼ, tᵢ − tⱼ ∈ ker φ, and this Čech 1-cocycle is the obstruction.
    (b) exp: O → O*, f ↦ e^f, is onto on stalks (on a small disc every
    non-vanishing function has a logarithm) with kernel 2πiℤ. The long
    exact sequence reads
       O(X) → O*(X) → H¹(X, 2πiℤ) → H¹(X, O) = 0,
    (X is Stein). H¹(ℂ*, ℤ) = ℤ, and the image of f is its winding number
    (1/2πi)∮ f′/f dz. For f = z: (1/2πi)∮ dz/z = 1 ≠ 0, so z = e^g has no
    solution. Concretely: cover ℂ* by U₁ = ℂ ∖ (−∞, 0], U₂ = ℂ ∖ [0, ∞);
    choose logs on each; on U₁ ∩ U₂ (two half planes) their difference is
    0 on one and 2πi on the other — the non-trivial cocycle. (c) The
    Zariski stalk at the generic point is ℂ(x) and x is not a square
    there (odd valuation at 0). So x ∈ O*(G_m) is not a square on ANY
    Zariski open. But f: G_m → G_m, t ↦ t² is finite étale (derivative 2t
    is a unit), so it is an étale cover, and f*x = t² is a square on it.
    This is why the KUMMER sequence 0 → μ₂ → G_m → G_m → 0 is exact only
    in the étale topology (D7).
    Common mistakes: checking surjectivity on global sections; forgetting
    that sheafification is needed for cokernels.
    Now you try: on X = ℂ, is exp: O(X) → O*(X) surjective? (yes: ℂ is
    simply connected, H¹(ℂ, ℤ) = 0)

D3. (a) State the valuative criteria of separatedness and properness.
    (b) Let X be the affine line with doubled origin. Show X is not
    separated, while A¹ is. (c) Show A¹_k → Spec k is not proper in two
    ways: by the valuative criterion and by exhibiting a closed set of
    A¹ × A¹ whose projection is not closed. How does P¹ fix this?
    Answer: (a) f: X → Y (finite type, noetherian) is separated (proper)
    iff every commutative square Spec K → X, Spec R → Y with R a DVR has
    at most one (exactly one) lift Spec R → X. (b) the map t ↦ t from the
    punctured disc has two limits. (c) x = 1/t has no limit;
    V(xy − 1) ↦ A¹ ∖ {0}; in P¹ × A¹ the closure adds [1 : 0] over y = 0.
    Teacher explains: (b) X = U₁ ∪ U₂, Uⱼ = Spec k[x], glued along
    A¹ ∖ {0} by the identity; it has two origins 0₁, 0₂. Take R =
    k[t]_(t), K = k(t), and Spec K → X given by x = t (the generic point
    of the line). Both Spec R → U₁ and Spec R → U₂ (x ↦ t) extend it, and
    they differ at the closed point (0₁ versus 0₂). Two extensions ⇒ not
    separated. Equivalently the diagonal Δ ⊂ X × X is not closed: its
    closure contains (0₁, 0₂). For A¹ a map Spec R → A¹ is an element of
    R, determined by its image in K, so the lift is unique. (c) Map Spec K
    → A¹ by x = 1/t. A lift would be x ∈ R = k[t]_(t), but 1/t ∉ R: no
    lift, so A¹ is not proper (it is separated: at most one lift).
    Directly: Z = V(xy − 1) ⊂ A¹_x × A¹_y is closed, and its image in
    A¹_y is {y ≠ 0}, not closed: A¹_x → Spec k is not universally closed.
    Homogenize in P¹ with x = x₀/x₁: Z̄ = V(x₀y − x₁) ⊂ P¹ × A¹_y. Over
    y = a ≠ 0 the point [1 : a]; over y = 0 the equation says x₁ = 0, the
    point [1 : 0] = ∞. So the image is all of A¹ — closed. In the
    valuative criterion [1/t : 1] = [1 : t] → [1 : 0] as t → 0.
    Common mistakes: confusing "separated" with "Hausdorff" (A¹ is
    separated but its Zariski topology is far from Hausdorff); forgetting
    that P¹ is ALSO two copies of A¹ glued, but by x ↦ 1/x.
    Now you try: is the affine plane with doubled origin separated? (no:
    the same two lifts through the line x₂ = 0 exist)

D4. (a) Define flat, unramified and étale. (b) Decide where each map is
    flat and where it is étale (char k ≠ 2 unless said):
       φ₁: Spec k[x, y]/(y² − x) → A¹_x;   φ₂: Spec k[x, y]/(xy) → A¹_x;
       φ₃: A¹_t → C = Spec k[t², t³] (normalization of the cusp);
       φ₄: Spec k[x, y]/(yᵖ − y − x) → A¹_x in char p.
    (c) What happens to φ₁ in characteristic 2?
    Answer: φ₁ finite flat of degree 2, étale exactly over x ≠ 0;
    φ₂ not flat at x = 0 (flat over x ≠ 0); φ₃ finite, birational, not flat
    at the cusp; φ₄ finite étale of degree p everywhere. (c) φ₁ is then
    nowhere étale (purely inseparable).
    Teacher explains: (a) f is FLAT if each O_{f(x)} → O_x is flat;
    UNRAMIFIED if Ω_{X/Y} = 0 (locally of finite type); ÉTALE = flat and
    unramified. For B = A[y]/(g), g monic: B is free over A, and
    Ω_{B/A} = B dy/(g′(y) dy) = B/(g′(y)), so B is étale exactly where
    g′(y) is invertible. (b) φ₁: B = k[x] ⊕ k[x]y is free of rank 2, so
    flat; g′ = 2y, étale where y ≠ 0, i.e. over x ≠ 0. Fibre over x = a:
    k[y]/(y² − a), two reduced points for a ≠ 0, one double point at a = 0
    (length still 2: flatness keeps the length constant). φ₂: in
    B = k[x, y]/(xy), x·y = 0 with y ≠ 0, so B has x-torsion; over the PID
    k[x] flat ⇔ torsion-free, so φ₂ is not flat. Geometrically the fibre
    over a ≠ 0 is a point and over 0 a whole line: the fibre dimension
    jumps. φ₃: k[t] is generated over A = k[t², t³] by 1, t. Fibre over
    the cusp (t², t³): k[t]/(t², t³) = k[t]/(t²), length 2; over a point
    s ≠ 0 of C the fibre is one reduced point, length 1. A finite flat
    module over a local ring is free, of constant rank, so φ₃ is not flat
    at the cusp — although it is finite, surjective and birational. φ₄:
    g = yᵖ − y − x has g′ = pyᵖ⁻¹ − 1 = −1, a unit: étale everywhere, and
    B = k[y] is a domain, so φ₄ is a CONNECTED finite étale cover of A¹ of
    degree p: π₁^ét(A¹) ≠ 1 in char p (in char 0, Riemann-Hurwitz forbids
    this: 2g − 2 = −2d + (ramification) with no ramification over A¹
    forces d = 1). (c) In char 2, g′ = 2y = 0, Ω = B dy ≠ 0 everywhere.
    Common mistakes: "finite + surjective ⇒ flat" (φ₃ is a counterexample);
    forgetting the characteristic when differentiating.
    Now you try: k[x] → k[x, y]/(y³ − x) in char 3 and in char 5.
    (char 3: nowhere étale since 3y² = 0; char 5: étale over x ≠ 0)

D5. (a) Compute Hⁱ(P¹, O(d)) for all d by Čech cohomology. (b) State
    Serre's theorem on Hⁱ(Pⁿ, O(d)) and compute h⁰(P³, O(2)), h³(P³, O(−6))
    and χ(P², O(−1)). (c) Show that U = A²_k ∖ {0} is not affine.
    Answer: (a) h⁰ = d + 1 (d ≥ −1, else 0), h¹ = −d − 1 (d ≤ −2, else 0).
    (b) 10, 10, 0. (c) H¹(U, O_U) is infinite-dimensional, with basis
    x^{−i}y^{−j}, i, j ≥ 1.
    Teacher explains: (a) Cover P¹ by U₀ = {x₀ ≠ 0} = Spec k[t] and U₁ =
    {x₁ ≠ 0} = Spec k[t⁻¹], t = x₁/x₀; U₀ ∩ U₁ = Spec k[t, t⁻¹]. Trivialize
    O(d) by x₀^d on U₀; on U₁ the sections are k[t⁻¹]·x₁^d = t^d k[t⁻¹]·x₀^d.
    So in Laurent monomials tʲ:
       C⁰ = k[t] ⊕ t^d k[t⁻¹]  (j ≥ 0 and j ≤ d),   C¹ = k[t, t⁻¹],
    with d(a, b) = b − a. H⁰ = kernel = monomials with 0 ≤ j ≤ d: d + 1 of
    them. H¹ = cokernel = monomials with d < j < 0: there are −d − 1 of
    them when d ≤ −2 (for d = −2 only t⁻¹). This is Serre duality h¹(O(d))
    = h⁰(O(−2 − d)) with ω_{P¹} = O(−2). (b) Hⁱ(Pⁿ, O(d)) = 0 for 0 < i <
    n, h⁰ = C(n + d, n) (d ≥ 0), hⁿ(O(d)) = h⁰(O(−d − n − 1)). So
    h⁰(P³, O(2)) = C(5, 3) = 10 (the ten quadratic monomials),
    h³(P³, O(−6)) = h⁰(O(6 − 4)) = 10, and χ(P², O(d)) = (d + 1)(d + 2)/2
    as a polynomial for all d, which vanishes at d = −1 (and d = −2).
    (c) Cover U by D(x) = Spec k[x, x⁻¹, y] and D(y) = Spec k[x, y, y⁻¹];
    D(x) ∩ D(y) = Spec k[x^{±1}, y^{±1}]. H⁰ = k[x, x⁻¹, y] ∩ k[x, y, y⁻¹]
    = k[x, y] (Hartogs), and
       H¹ = k[x^{±1}, y^{±1}]/(k[x^{±1}, y] + k[x, y^{±1}])
    has basis x^{−i}y^{−j} with i, j ≥ 1 (monomials negative in BOTH
    variables): infinite-dimensional. An affine scheme has Hⁱ(qcoh) = 0 for
    i > 0, so U is not affine. (Also: if U were affine with O(U) = k[x,
    y], the inclusion U → A² would be an isomorphism — but 0 ∉ U.)
    Common mistakes: using C(n + d, n) for negative d (it is the
    polynomial, not the dimension); forgetting Hⁱ, 0 < i < n, vanish.
    Now you try: h²(P², O(−5)). (h⁰(O(2)) = 6)

D6. (a) For a smooth plane curve C of degree d use 0 → O_{P²}(−d) → O_{P²}
    → O_C → 0 to compute h⁰(O_C) and the genus g = h¹(O_C). (b) For a
    smooth plane quartic find ω_C by adjunction, and compute h⁰(O_C(1)),
    h⁰(O_C(2)), checking with Riemann-Roch. (c) Compute the Hilbert
    polynomials of two skew lines and of two meeting lines in P³, and
    explain why a flat family of skew lines degenerating to meeting lines
    must acquire an embedded point.
    Answer: (a) h⁰ = 1, g = (d − 1)(d − 2)/2. (b) ω_C = O_C(1); 3 and 6.
    (c) 2t + 2 and 2t + 1: the flat limit has an embedded point at the
    intersection (a planar pair of lines plus a point sticking out of the
    plane).
    Teacher explains: (a) The long exact sequence:
       0 → H⁰(O(−d)) = 0 → H⁰(O) = k → H⁰(O_C) → H¹(O(−d)) = 0,
       0 = H¹(O) → H¹(O_C) → H²(O(−d)) → H²(O) = 0.
    So h⁰(O_C) = 1 (C is connected) and g = h²(P², O(−d)) = h⁰(O(d − 3))
    = C(d − 1, 2) = (d − 1)(d − 2)/2: d = 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 give 0, 0, 1, 3, 6.
    (b) ω_C = (ω_{P²} ⊗ O(C))|_C = O(−3 + 4)|_C = O_C(1), of degree 4 =
    2g − 2 ✓ (g = 3). Twisting the sequence: 0 → O(−3) → O(1) → O_C(1) →
    0, and H⁰(O(−3)) = H¹(O(−3)) = 0 give h⁰(O_C(1)) = h⁰(O(1)) = 3 = g,
    as it must for ω_C. For O_C(2): 0 → O(−2) → O(2) → O_C(2) → 0 gives
    h⁰ = 6 − 0 = 6 (H¹(O(−2)) = 0). Riemann-Roch: deg 8 > 2g − 2 = 4, so
    h¹ = 0 and h⁰ = 8 + 1 − 3 = 6 ✓. (c) A line L has P_L(t) = t + 1. Two
    disjoint lines: O_{L∪M} = O_L ⊕ O_M, P = 2t + 2. Two lines meeting at
    a point p lie in a plane and form a conic xy = 0: P = 2t + 1 (from
    0 → O(t − 2) → O_{P²}(t) → O_{conic}(t) → 0: C(t + 2, 2) − C(t, 2) =
    2t + 1). The constant term is χ(O) = 1 − p_a: the pair of meeting lines
    has p_a = 0, the skew pair χ = 2. By the flatness criterion the flat
    limit of Lₛ ∪ M (s → 0) must have P = 2t + 2, so it is the planar pair
    plus ONE extra dimension of functions at p: an embedded point pointing
    out of the plane (Hartshorne III.9.8.4).
    Common mistakes: using the degree-genus formula for singular curves
    without subtracting δ (a nodal cubic has geometric genus 0 but
    arithmetic genus 1); forgetting that h⁰(ω_C) = g, not 2g − 2.
    Now you try: the genus of a smooth complete intersection of a quadric
    and a cubic in P³. (ω = O_C(2 + 3 − 4) = O_C(1), deg 6 = 2g − 2, g = 4)

D7. (a) Show that for an irreducible scheme X and a constant sheaf A,
    Hⁱ_Zar(X, A) = 0 for i > 0; explain why this motivates the étale
    topology. (b) Prove that the Kummer sequence 0 → μ_n → G_m →(n) G_m →
    0 is exact on X_ét when n is invertible on X, and deduce Hⁱ_ét(C, μ_n)
    for a smooth projective connected curve C of genus g over k = k̄.
    (c) Compute H¹_ét(G_{m,k̄}, μ_n), H¹(Spec F_q, ℤ/n), H¹(Spec ℝ, μ₂).
    Answer: (b) H⁰ = μ_n, H¹ = Pic(C)[n] ≅ (ℤ/n)^{2g}, H² ≅ ℤ/n, Hⁱ = 0 for
    i ≥ 3. (c) ℤ/n, ℤ/n, ℤ/2.
    Teacher explains: (a) On an irreducible space every non-empty open set
    is connected and dense, so A(U) = A for U ≠ ∅ and all restriction
    maps are isomorphisms: A is FLASQUE, hence acyclic. So a smooth
    projective curve of genus 5 over ℂ has H¹_Zar(C, ℤ) = 0, while
    H¹(C(ℂ), ℤ) = ℤ¹⁰. Zariski opens are too big: we need "small" opens
    that are not subsets — étale maps U → X, the algebraic substitute for
    local homeomorphisms. (b) Exactness at the right: given a unit u on
    U, the map U′ = Spec O_U[T]/(Tⁿ − u) → U is finite étale (derivative
    nTⁿ⁻¹ is a unit as n and u are units) and surjective, and u = Tⁿ is
    an n-th power on U′. The kernel of u ↦ uⁿ is μ_n by definition. Use
    HILBERT 90: H¹_ét(X, G_m) = Pic X. The long exact sequence for C:
       0 → μ_n(k) → k* →(n) k* → H¹(μ_n) → Pic C →(n) Pic C → H²(μ_n)
         → Br C = 0,
    where O*(C) = k* (C proper, connected, reduced). k = k̄ makes k* →(n)
    k* onto, so H¹(C, μ_n) = Pic(C)[n]. Pic C = ℤ ⊕ Pic⁰ C with Pic⁰ C =
    J(k), J an abelian variety of dimension g, so Pic(C)[n] = J[n] ≅
    (ℤ/n)^{2g}. Pic⁰ is divisible, so Pic C/n = ℤ/n via the degree mod n,
    and Br C = 0 (Tsen's theorem) gives H²(C, μ_n) ≅ ℤ/n; cd ≤ 2 dim C
    kills the rest. These match b₀ = 1, b₁ = 2g, b₂ = 1 of a genus-g
    surface — Artin's comparison theorem. (c) G_m = Spec k̄[t, t⁻¹]: O* =
    k̄*·t^ℤ, and modulo n-th powers only t survives: ℤ/n; Pic G_m = 0, so
    H¹ = ℤ/n, the class of the Kummer cover s ↦ sⁿ. For a field,
    H¹(k, ℤ/n) = Hom_cont(Gal(k^sep/k), ℤ/n), and Gal(F̄_q/F_q) = ℤ̂
    (Frobenius), so Hom(ℤ̂, ℤ/n) = ℤ/n. Kummer for ℝ: H¹(ℝ, μ₂) = ℝ*/ℝ*²
    = {±1} = ℤ/2.
    Common mistakes: using Kummer when n is divisible by the
    characteristic (H¹(A¹_{F̄_p}, ℤ/p) = k[x]/{fᵖ − f} is infinite, by
    Artin-Schreier); identifying μ_n with ℤ/n over non-closed fields (the
    Galois actions differ: Tate twist).
    Now you try: H¹_ét of P¹_ℂ minus 3 points with ℤ/ℓ coefficients.
    ((ℤ/ℓ)², rank 2g + r − 1 = 0 + 3 − 1)

D8. (a) State the Weil conjectures and the Lefschetz trace formula for a
    smooth projective X over F_q. (b) For E: y² = x³ + x + 1 over F₅ count
    #E(F₅), write Z(E, t), find the Frobenius eigenvalues and #E(F₂₅).
    (c) Count #G(2, 4)(F_q) and read off the ℓ-adic Betti numbers.
    Answer: (b) 9 points, a = −3, Z = (1 + 3t + 5t²)/((1 − t)(1 − 5t)),
    α, β = (−3 ± i√11)/2 with |α| = √5, #E(F₂₅) = 27. (c) (q² + 1)(q² +
    q + 1) = q⁴ + q³ + 2q² + q + 1; b₀, b₂, b₄, b₆, b₈ = 1, 1, 2, 1, 1, odd
    Betti numbers 0.
    Teacher explains: (a) Let Nₘ = #X(F_{qᵐ}) and Z(X, t) = exp(∑ Nₘtᵐ/m).
    For X of dimension n: Z is RATIONAL, Z = ∏ Pᵢ(t)^{(−1)^{i+1}} with
    Pᵢ(t) = det(1 − tF | Hⁱ_ét(X_{F̄_q}, ℚ_ℓ)); FUNCTIONAL EQUATION
    Z(1/(qⁿt)) = ±q^{nχ/2}t^χ Z(t); RIEMANN HYPOTHESIS: the eigenvalues of
    F on Hⁱ have |α| = q^{i/2} (Deligne 1974); deg Pᵢ = bᵢ. The TRACE
    FORMULA Nₘ = ∑(−1)ⁱ Tr(Fᵐ | Hⁱ) gives rationality at once. (b) f(x) =
    x³ + x + 1 mod 5 (squares mod 5 are 0, 1, 4):
       x = 0: 1 → y = ±1, 2 points;  x = 1: 3, non-square, 0 points;
       x = 2: 11 ≡ 1 → 2 points;     x = 3: 31 ≡ 1 → 2 points;
       x = 4: 69 ≡ 4 → 2 points;     plus the point at infinity.
    N₁ = 9, a = q + 1 − N₁ = 6 − 9 = −3. Hasse: |a| ≤ 2√5 ≈ 4.47 ✓. P₁(t)
    = 1 − at + qt² = 1 + 3t + 5t², so Z = (1 + 3t + 5t²)/((1 − t)(1 −
    5t)). Its reciprocal roots satisfy T² + 3T + 5 = 0: α = (−3 + i√11)/2,
    β = ᾱ, |α|² = (9 + 11)/4 = 5 ✓. Then α² + β² = (α + β)² − 2αβ = 9 −
    10 = −1 and N₂ = 25 + 1 − (−1) = 27. (c) G(2, 4)(F_q) = pairs of
    independent vectors in F_q⁴ modulo GL₂:
       (q⁴ − 1)(q⁴ − q)/((q² − 1)(q² − q)) = (q² + 1)(q² + q + 1).
    Expanding gives q⁴ + q³ + 2q² + q + 1. Since F acts on H^{2i} by qⁱ for
    such a cellular variety, b_{2i} is the coefficient of qⁱ: 1, 1, 2, 1,
    1; χ_top = 6 = C(4, 2), the number of Schubert cells.
    Common mistakes: forgetting the point at infinity; using q + 1 + a
    instead of q + 1 − a; thinking Nₘ = N₁ᵐ.
    Now you try: E: y² = x³ + x over F₅: N₁ and N₂. (N₁ = 4, a = 2,
    N₂ = 26 − (4 − 10) = 32)

D9. (a) Explain what it means for G(k, n) to be a fine moduli space and
    compute its tangent space at [V]. (b) The Hilbert scheme has tangent
    space H⁰(Z, N_Z) at a smooth (lci) Z with obstructions in H¹(N_Z).
    Compute its dimension at a line, a plane conic and a twisted cubic in
    P³. (c) Show that elliptic curves have no fine moduli space, and
    describe the coarse space M_{1,1} → A¹.
    Answer: (a) G(k, n) represents S ↦ {rank-k subbundles of O_Sⁿ (with
    locally free quotient)}; T = Hom(V, kⁿ/V), dim k(n − k). (b) 4, 8, 12.
    (c) an isotrivial family (quadratic twist) is non-trivial; j: M_{1,1}
    → A¹ is a bijection on k̄-points.
    Teacher explains: (a) h_G(S) = Hom(S, G) ≅ {subbundles V ⊂ O_Sⁿ}; the
    UNIVERSAL family is the tautological S ⊂ O_Gⁿ. A tangent vector is a
    map Spec k[ε] → G, i.e. a subspace V_ε ⊂ k[ε]ⁿ free of rank k lifting
    V: write it as the graph of a map V → kⁿ/V (the ε-part). So T =
    Hom(V, kⁿ/V) and dim G = k(n − k); G(2, 4) has dim 4. (b) For Z ⊂
    P³ smooth, N = T_{P³}|_Z / T_Z. LINE L: N = O(1) ⊕ O(1), h⁰ = 4 =
    dim G(2, 4) ✓, h¹ = 0. PLANE CONIC C in a plane H: N = N_{C/H} ⊕
    N_{H/P³}|_C = O_{P¹}(4) ⊕ O_{P¹}(2) (C ≅ P¹, O(1)|_C = O_{P¹}(2)),
    h⁰ = 5 + 3 = 8: choose the plane (3) and a conic in it (5). TWISTED
    CUBIC: deg N = deg T_{P³}|_C − deg T_C = 4·3 − 2 = 10, and in fact N ≅
    O(5) ⊕ O(5), h⁰ = 12, h¹ = 0: the Hilbert scheme is smooth of dim 12
    there — matching PGL₄ (dim 15) modulo Aut P¹ = PGL₂ (dim 3). (c) If
    M were fine, a family E → S with all fibres isomorphic would have a
    constant classifying map S → M and so be pulled back from a point,
    i.e. trivial. But over S = G_m = Spec k[t, t⁻¹] (char ≠ 2) take the
    twist E_t: t y² = x³ + ax + b of a fixed E. Every fibre is ≅ E over k̄
    (y ↦ y/√t), yet the family becomes trivial only after the double
    cover s² = t: it is a non-trivial torsor under Aut E ⊇ {±1}. The
    culprit is the automorphism −1 of every elliptic curve. The
    j-invariant j = 1728·4a³/(4a³ + 27b²) classifies curves over k̄: the
    COARSE space is A¹_j; the stack M_{1,1} remembers the automorphisms
    (generic ±1, extra ones at j = 0 (order 6) and j = 1728 (order 4)).
    Common mistakes: counting a twisted cubic as "3 × 4 parameters";
    thinking a coarse space carries a universal family.
    Now you try: dim of the Hilbert scheme of plane cubics in P³ at a
    smooth one. (3 + 9 = 12)

D10. (a) Show that first-order deformations of a smooth projective curve
    C are classified by H¹(C, T_C) and that they are unobstructed; compute
    h⁰(T_C), h¹(T_C) for g = 0, 1, 2, 3 and deduce dim M_g = 3g − 3 for
    g ≥ 2. (b) By parameter counts show that the general curve of genus 3
    is a plane quartic but the general curve of genus 6 is not a plane
    quintic, and find the dimension of the hyperelliptic locus in M_g.
    (c) Find the number of moduli of smooth cubic surfaces and of quintic
    threefolds.
    Answer: (a) (h⁰, h¹) = (3, 0), (1, 1), (0, 3), (0, 6). (b) 14 − 8 = 6
    = dim M₃; 20 − 8 = 12 < 15 = dim M₆; hyperelliptic locus 2g − 1.
    (c) 4 and 101.
    Teacher explains: (a) Cover C by affines Uᵢ; each Uᵢ is smooth affine,
    so it has no non-trivial first-order deformations; a deformation over
    k[ε] is glued from the trivial ones Uᵢ × Spec k[ε] by automorphisms
    1 + εθᵢⱼ with θᵢⱼ ∈ T_C(Uᵢⱼ) (derivations). These form a Čech
    1-cocycle, changes of trivialization a coboundary: DEFORMATIONS =
    H¹(C, T_C). Obstructions lie in H²(C, T_C) = 0 (dim 1). Riemann-Roch
    for T_C (deg 2 − 2g): h⁰ − h¹ = (2 − 2g) + 1 − g = 3 − 3g.
       g = 0: T = O(2): h⁰ = 3 (sl₂), h¹ = 0.
       g = 1: T = O: h⁰ = h¹ = 1.
       g ≥ 2: deg T < 0, h⁰ = 0, h¹ = 3g − 3: g = 2: 3; g = 3: 6.
    For g ≥ 2 the curve has finitely many automorphisms, and dim M_g =
    h¹ = 3g − 3. (b) Plane curves of degree d: P^{d(d+3)/2}, modulo PGL₃
    (dim 8). d = 4: 14 − 8 = 6 = 3·3 − 3, so plane quartics fill an open
    set of M₃ (the non-hyperelliptic curves, via the canonical map, since
    ω_C = O_C(1)). d = 5 (g = 6): 20 − 8 = 12 < 15: plane quintics form a
    proper subvariety. HYPERELLIPTIC: C → P¹ of degree 2 has, by
    Riemann-Hurwitz 2g − 2 = 2(−2) + b, b = 2g + 2 branch points; choose
    them on P¹ modulo PGL₂: 2g + 2 − 3 = 2g − 1. For g = 2 this is 3 =
    dim M₂ (all genus 2 curves are hyperelliptic). (c) Cubic surfaces:
    C(6, 3) − 1 = 19 parameters, minus dim PGL₄ = 15: 4. Quintic
    threefolds: C(9, 4) − 1 = 125, minus dim PGL₅ = 24: 101 = h¹(T_X) =
    h^{2,1}(X).
    Common mistakes: forgetting to subtract the automorphism group;
    applying 3g − 3 for g = 0, 1 (M₀ is a point with automorphisms PGL₂;
    M_{1,1} has dim 1).
    Now you try: the number of moduli of smooth quartic surfaces in P³.
    (C(7, 3) − 1 − 15 = 34 − 15 = 19)

D11. (a) State Bézout's theorem via A*(P²) and compute the intersection
    form and the genus of a smooth curve of class (a, b) on Q = P¹ × P¹.
    (b) Let S be P² blown up at six general points (a smooth cubic
    surface). Compute K_S², find all classes C = dH − ∑aᵢEᵢ with C² = −1,
    K·C = −1, and count them. (c) Using Schubert calculus on G(2, 4) show
    that four general lines in P³ are met by exactly 2 lines.
    Answer: (a) (a, b)·(c, d) = ad + bc; g = (a − 1)(b − 1). (b) K² = 3;
    6 classes Eᵢ, 15 classes H − Eᵢ − Eⱼ, 6 classes 2H − ∑_{k≠i}E_k:
    27. (c) σ₁⁴ = 2.
    Teacher explains: (a) A*(P²) = ℤ[H]/(H³): curves of degrees d, e
    meet in de points with multiplicity (a conic and a cubic: 6). On Q,
    Pic = ℤf₁ ⊕ ℤf₂ (the two rulings) with f₁² = f₂² = 0, f₁·f₂ = 1, so
    (a, b)·(c, d) = ad + bc. K_Q = (−2, −2) (restrict K_{P¹} from each
    factor). Adjunction for C = (a, b):
       2g − 2 = C² + K·C = 2ab − 2a − 2b, so g = (a − 1)(b − 1).
    The (2, 2) curves (quadric ∩ quadric) have g = 1; the twisted cubic is
    (1, 2), g = 0; (3, 3) = quadric ∩ cubic: g = 4 (as in D6). (b) On S:
    H² = 1, Eᵢ² = −1, H·Eᵢ = Eᵢ·Eⱼ = 0 (i ≠ j), K = −3H + ∑Eᵢ, so K² = 9 −
    6 = 3 = deg S in P³. For C = dH − ∑aᵢEᵢ:
       C² = d² − ∑aᵢ² = −1,   K·C = −3d + ∑aᵢ = −1.
    Cauchy-Schwarz: (3d − 1)² = (∑aᵢ)² ≤ 6∑aᵢ² = 6(d² + 1), i.e. 3d² −
    6d − 5 ≤ 0, so 0 ≤ d ≤ 2. d = 0: one aᵢ = −1: Eᵢ (6). d = 1: ∑aᵢ = 2,
    ∑aᵢ² = 2: two aᵢ = 1: lines through two points (15). d = 2: ∑aᵢ = 5 =
    ∑aᵢ²: five aᵢ = 1: conics through five points (6). Total 6 + 15 + 6 =
    27; by adjunction each is a smooth rational curve with −K·C = 1, i.e.
    a LINE in the anticanonical embedding S ⊂ P³. (c) In A*(G(2, 4)) the
    lines meeting a fixed line form the divisor σ₁. PIERI: σ₁·σ₁ = σ₂ +
    σ₁₁ (lines through a point; lines in a plane). σ₂² = 1 (one line
    through two points), σ₁₁² = 1 (one line in two planes), σ₂σ₁₁ = 0
    (a line through a general point p lying in a general plane H would
    need p ∈ H). Hence σ₁⁴ = (σ₂ + σ₁₁)² = 1 + 0 + 0 + 1 = 2. Geometric check:
    three general lines lie on a unique smooth quadric (one ruling); the
    fourth line meets the quadric in 2 points, and the lines of the other
    ruling through them are the 2 answers.
    Common mistakes: forgetting to check that intersections are
    transverse; K² = 9 − r only for blow-ups of P² at r points.
    Now you try: the degree of G(2, 4) ⊂ P⁵ in the Plücker embedding.
    (σ₁⁴ = 2: it is a quadric)

D12. (a) Compute the local intersection multiplicity at 0 of the cusp
    y² = x³ with: y = 0; x = 0; the node y² = x² + x³. (b) Let X = {x = y =
    0} ∪ {z = w = 0} ⊂ A⁴ and Y = {x = z, y = w}. Compute length(O_X ⊗
    O_Y) and Serre's Tor formula at 0. (c) Compute the derived
    self-intersection {0} ×^h_{A¹} {0}, its virtual dimension, and explain
    "27 = ∫c₄(Sym³S^∨)" as a virtual count on a derived zero locus.
    Answer: (a) 3, 2, 4. (b) length 3, Tor₁ of length 1, higher Tor 0,
    multiplicity 3 − 1 = 2. (c) Spec(k ⊗^L_{k[x]} k) with π₀ = k, π₁ = k:
    vdim 0 + 0 − 1 = −1.
    Teacher explains: (a) I₀(f, g) = dim_k k[x, y]_{(x,y)}/(f, g).
    y = 0: k[x]/(x³): 3. x = 0: k[y]/(y²): 2. Node: (y² − x³) − (y² −
    x² − x³) = x², so the ideal is (x², y² − x³) = (x², y²) (x³ ∈ (x²)),
    of dimension 4 (basis 1, x, y, xy). Check: I ≥ m(f)m(g) = 2·2 = 4,
    with equality because the tangent cone of the cusp (y² = 0) and of
    the node (y = ±x) share no line. (b) Put I = (x, y) ∩ (z, w) = (xz,
    xw, yz, yw). Modulo (x − z, y − w) set z = x, w = y: I becomes
    (x², xy, y²), so O_X ⊗ O_Y = k[x, y]/(x, y)², of length 3. But X is two
    planes, each meeting the plane Y transversally at 0, so the "right"
    answer is 1 + 1 = 2. Serre: Y is cut out by the regular sequence u = x
    − z, v = y − w, so Torᵢ(O_X, O_Y) = Hᵢ(Koszul(u, v; O_X)). u is a
    non-zero-divisor on O_X (it is ±a coordinate on each plane), so H₂ = 0,
    and the Koszul Euler characteristic equals the multiplicity e(u, v;
    O_X) = 1 + 1 = 2. Hence length Tor₁ = 3 − 2 = 1 and ∑(−1)ⁱ length Torᵢ =
    2. The failure is because O_X is not Cohen-Macaulay (depth 1, dim 2).
    (c) Resolve k by the Koszul complex 0 → k[x] →(x) k[x] → k → 0 and
    tensor with k: 0 → k →(0) k → 0, so Tor₀ = k, Tor₁ = k. The derived
    intersection is Spec of the dg algebra k[ε], deg ε = 1 (homological),
    dε = 0 — the classical point plus an "obstruction". Its virtual
    dimension is dim + dim − dim ambient = 0 + 0 − 1 = −1, as the
    Euler characteristic 1 − 1 = 0 of the virtual structure sheaf shows
    (the virtual class in A_{−1} is 0: a point moved off itself does not
    meet itself). In general the derived zero locus Z(s) of a section of
    a rank r bundle F on smooth M has vdim = dim M − r and virtual class
    c_r(F) ∩ [M], whatever the actual zero set. Lines on a cubic surface
    X = V(f): f gives a section of Sym³S^∨ (rank 4) on G(2, 4) (dim 4),
    vanishing at the lines in X; vdim = 0 and ∫c₄(Sym³S^∨) = 27.
    Common mistakes: computing only Tor₀; forgetting that I₀ is computed in
    the LOCAL ring (other intersection points must be discarded).
    Now you try: I₀(y² − x³, y² − x⁵). ((y² − x³, x³ − x⁵) = (y², x³)
    locally since 1 − x² is a unit: 6)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------

      g) 2 points (ℂ[x]/(x² + 1) ≅ ℂ × ℂ)  h) 2 points (F₄ × F₄)
      i) 2 points: x³ − 2 = (x − ∛2)(x² + ∛2x + ∛4) over ℚ(∛2)
      j) empty
      k) 3 points: (x − 1), (x + 1), (x² + 1)
      l) x³ − x = x(x + 1)²: 2 points, not reduced
      m) real points (κ = ℝ) and conjugate pairs {z, z̄}, Im z > 0 (κ = ℂ)

      q) 3, 2, 2

    h) I₀(x² − y³, y² − x³)             i) I₀(y − x², y − x³)
    j) Torᵢ^{k[x]}(k[x]/(x²), k[x]/(x³)), i = 0, 1
    k) Torᵢ^{k[x,y]}(k[x,y]/(y), k[x,y]/(y))
    l) Torᵢ^{k[x,y]}(k[x,y]/(x), k[x,y]/(y))
    m) Torᵢ^{k[x,y]}(k, k) and the vdim of a point ×^h_{A²} a point
    n) Serre's example (D12 b): length Tor₀, Tor₁, multiplicity
    o) cotangent complex of V(x²) ⊂ A¹: dim H⁻¹, H⁰ (char ≠ 2)
    p) virtual count of the derived self-intersection of a plane curve
       of degree d in P²
    q) χ(O(a), O(b)) on P¹; Ext¹(O(1), O(−1)) and the extension it gives
    r) Ext²_{P²}(O, O(−3))
    Answers:
      a) 2          b) 5          c) 2          d) 3, 2, 2
      e) 3 (y = x is a branch tangent), 2      f) 4
      g) ∞ (common component: y² − x⁴ = (y − x²)(y + x²))
      h) 4          i) 2 ((y − x², x²(1 − x)))
      j) Tor₀ = k[x]/(x²), Tor₁ = k[x]/(x²) (x³ acts by 0): χ = 0
      k) Tor₀ = Tor₁ = k[x] (the conormal bundle), Tor₂ = 0
      l) Tor₀ = k, higher Tor = 0
      m) k, k², k (Koszul); vdim 0 + 0 − 2 = −2
      n) 3, 1, 2    o) 1, 1 (H⁻¹ = (x)/(x²), H⁰ = Ω = k dx)
      p) C² = d²    q) b − a + 1; k: 0 → O(−1) → O² → O(1) → 0
      r) H²(O(−3)) = k (Serre duality: ω = O(−3))

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) Every scheme is affine.
     b) Spec k[x]/(x²) and Spec k have the same underlying space.
     c) A map of sheaves onto on every stalk is onto on global sections.
     d) Every finite morphism is proper.
     e) Every finite surjective morphism of varieties is flat.
     f) Étale morphisms are open.
     g) Hⁱ(X, F) = 0 for i > 0, F quasi-coherent, X affine.
     h) A² ∖ {0} is affine.
     i) Every smooth projective genus-3 curve is a plane quartic.
     j) H¹_Zar(C, ℤ) ≅ ℤ⁴ for a genus-2 curve C over ℂ.
     k) H¹_ét(A¹_{F̄_p}, ℤ/p) = 0.
     l) #E(F_q) ≤ q + 1 + 2√q for every elliptic curve over F_q.
     m) The functor of elliptic curves is representable by a scheme.
     n) dim M_g = 3g − 3 for every g ≥ 0.
     o) A flat projective family over a connected base has constant
        Hilbert polynomial.
     p) Four general lines in P³ have exactly two common transversals.
     q) I_P(f, g) = m_P(f)m_P(g) always.
     r) For a proper intersection, length(O_X ⊗ O_Y) is the
        intersection multiplicity.
     s) Every smooth cubic surface over ℂ contains exactly 27 lines.
     t) A general quartic surface in P³ contains a line.
     u) The derived self-intersection of a point in A¹ is a point.
     v) Pic(Spec ℤ[√−5]) = 0.
     Answers:
       a) F: P¹ (H¹(O(−2)) ≠ 0)         b) T
       c) F: exp: O → O* on ℂ* (D2)     d) T
       e) F: normalization of the cusp  f) T (flat, finite presentation)
       g) T (Serre)                      h) F: D5 c)
       i) F: hyperelliptic curves y² = x⁸ − 1
       j) F: 0 (constant sheaves are flasque)
       k) F: Artin-Schreier covers
       l) T (Hasse)    m) F: quadratic twists (D9)
       n) F: g = 0 (a point) and g = 1 (dim M_{1,1} = 1)
       o) T            p) T      q) F: I₀(y, y − x²) = 2 > 1
       r) F: Serre's two planes (3 ≠ 2)  s) T
       t) F: vdim 4 − 5 = −1            u) F: π₁ = k ≠ 0
       v) F: ℤ/2 ((2, 1 + √−5) is not principal)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: always draw the picture first — Mumford's arithmetic
  surface for Spec ℤ[x], fibres as "vertical lines" with fat points where
  ramification happens — and reduce every scheme question to commutative
  algebra of a ring and its primes. For cohomology, drill three moves
  until automatic: Serre's table for Pⁿ, the ideal-sheaf sequence 0 → O(−d)
  → O → O_X → 0, and Serre duality with ω = O(−n − 1) twisted by
  adjunction; every genus, h⁰ and Hilbert polynomial in this topic comes
  from these. Before any moduli count, subtract the automorphism group;
  before any intersection count, check properness and transversality,
  and when in doubt compute Tor. Keep a "sanity table" of Betti numbers
  and point counts (Pⁿ, curves, G(2, 4), cubic surfaces) — the Weil
  conjectures tie them together. Harder: prove the valuative criterion
  for Pⁿ; compute Hⁱ(Pⁿ, O(d)) in full by Čech; prove Serre duality for
  Pⁿ and Riemann-Roch on curves from it; derive H¹_ét(C, μ_n) for C a
  curve over F_q (not k̄) via Hochschild-Serre; prove σ₁⁴ = 2 by a
  degeneration argument and compute ∫c₄(Sym³S^∨) = 27 by the splitting
  principle; compute the normal bundle of the twisted cubic; and verify
  Serre's Tor formula on the two-planes example by writing out the
  Koszul complex explicitly.


================================================================================
TOPIC 13.2 — ARITHMETIC GEOMETRY: ELLIPTIC CURVES, ABELIAN VARIETIES,
MODULAR FORMS, GALOIS REPRESENTATIONS, THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - WEIERSTRASS EQUATIONS: E : y² + a₁xy + a₃y = x³ + a₂x² + a₄x + a₆, with
    b₂ = a₁² + 4a₂, b₄ = 2a₄ + a₁a₃, b₆ = a₃² + 4a₆, c₄ = b₂² − 24b₄, c₆ =
    −b₂³ + 36b₂b₄ − 216b₆, 1728Δ = c₄³ − c₆², j = c₄³/Δ. E is smooth iff
    Δ ≠ 0. For y² = x³ + ax + b: Δ = −16(4a³ + 27b²), j = 1728·4a³/(4a³ +
    27b²) = −1728(4a)³/Δ. Changes x = u²x′ + r, y = u³y′ + ... give
    Δ′ = u⁻¹²Δ, c₄′ = u⁻⁴c₄; j is invariant and classifies E over K̄.
    j = 0 (y² = x³ + b) and j = 1728 (y² = x³ + ax) have extra
    automorphisms; TWISTS are the forms over K with the same j.
  - GROUP LAW (chord and tangent, O = point at infinity): P + Q + R = O
    iff P, Q, R are collinear. E(K) is an abelian group; [n] has degree
    n², E[n] ≅ (ℤ/n)² when char K ∤ n. ISOGENIES φ : E → E′ have a DUAL
    φ̂ with φ̂φ = [deg φ]; the WEIL PAIRING eₙ : E[n] × E[n] → μₙ is
    bilinear, alternating, non-degenerate and Galois-equivariant.
  - OVER C: E(C) ≅ C/Λ via z ↦ (℘(z), ℘′(z)/2); every j ∈ C occurs,
    j(i) = 1728, j(e^{2πi/3}) = 0, j(τ) = q⁻¹ + 744 + 196884q + ... .
    End(E) is ℤ or an order in an imaginary quadratic field (COMPLEX
    MULTIPLICATION, CM).
  - OVER F_q: HASSE #E(F_q) = q + 1 − a, |a| ≤ 2√q; Frobenius satisfies
    φ² − aφ + q = 0; Z(E, T) = (1 − aT + qT²)/((1 − T)(1 − qT)) and
    #E(F_{q^k}) = q^k + 1 − αᵏ − βᵏ. E(F_q) ≅ ℤ/m × ℤ/mn. SUPERSINGULAR
    iff p | a (for p ≥ 5: a = 0 over F_p); DEURING: the number of
    supersingular j over F̄_p is ⌊p/12⌋ + (0, 1, 1, 2) for p ≡ (1, 5, 7,
    11) mod 12, and equals genus(X₀(p)) + 1.
  - OVER Q: MORDELL-WEIL E(Q) ≅ ℤ^r ⊕ E(Q)_tors. NAGELL-LUTZ: for y² =
    x³ + ax + b (a, b ∈ ℤ) a torsion point ≠ O has x, y ∈ ℤ and y = 0 or
    y² | 4a³ + 27b². MAZUR: E(Q)_tors is ℤ/n (1 ≤ n ≤ 10 or n = 12) or
    ℤ/2 × ℤ/2m (1 ≤ m ≤ 4). REDUCTION: E(Q)_tors ↪ Ẽ(F_p) for good odd p
    (for p = 2 only the odd part).
  - REDUCTION AT p (minimal model): GOOD (p ∤ Δ_min), MULTIPLICATIVE (node;
    SPLIT if tangent slopes lie in F_p, a_p = 1, else NON-SPLIT, a_p =
    −1), ADDITIVE (cusp, a_p = 0). SEMISTABLE = no additive primes.
    CONDUCTOR N = ∏p^{f_p}, f_p = 0, 1, 2 (good, mult., additive; p ≥ 5)
    and f₂ ≤ 8, f₃ ≤ 5. v_p(j) < 0 ⇔ potentially multiplicative (TATE
    CURVE); j ∈ ℤ_p ⇔ potentially good. NÉRON-OGG-SHAFAREVICH: good
    reduction at p ⇔ T_ℓE unramified at p (ℓ ≠ p).
  - DESCENT: 0 → E(Q)/nE(Q) → Sel⁽ⁿ⁾(E/Q) → Ш(E/Q)[n] → 0. Via a
    2-isogeny for y² = x³ + ax² + bx (E′: y² = x³ − 2ax² + (a² − 4b)x):
    2^r = #α(E(Q))·#α′(E′(Q))/4, α(x, y) = x mod Q*², α(0, 0) = b; d ∈
    α(E(Q)) iff N² = dM⁴ + aM²e² + (b/d)e⁴ has a solution with M ≠ 0.
  - L-FUNCTION: L(E, s) = ∏_{p∤N}(1 − a_p p⁻ˢ + p^{1−2s})⁻¹ ∏_{p|N}(1 −
    a_p p⁻ˢ)⁻¹, a_p = p + 1 − #Ẽ(F_p). Λ(s) = N^{s/2}(2π)⁻ˢΓ(s)L(E, s) =
    w Λ(2 − s), w = ±1 (for prime N, w = a_N).
  - BSD: ord_{s=1}L(E, s) = r and L^{(r)}(E, 1)/r! = Ω·Reg·#Ш·∏c_p /
    #E(Q)_tors². Known when the analytic rank is ≤ 1 (GROSS-ZAGIER +
    KOLYVAGIN; then Ш is finite). CONGRUENT NUMBERS: n is congruent iff
    y² = x³ − n²x has rank ≥ 1; TUNNELL's criterion (converse needs BSD).
  - ABELIAN VARIETIES: complete connected group varieties, automatically
    commutative and projective. dim g ⇒ A[n] ≅ (ℤ/n)^{2g}, deg[n] = n^{2g},
    T_ℓA ≅ ℤ_ℓ^{2g}. Over C: A(C) = C^g/Λ, and a complex torus is
    algebraic iff it has a RIEMANN FORM (positive-definite Hermitian H
    with Im H(Λ, Λ) ⊆ ℤ); for g ≥ 2 most tori are not. DUAL A^∨,
    POLARIZATIONS A → A^∨; POINCARÉ REDUCIBILITY: A ~ ∏Aᵢ^{nᵢ} (simple),
    End⁰(A) semisimple. MORDELL-WEIL: A(K) finitely generated.
  - JACOBIANS: J(C) of a genus-g curve, dimension g, principally
    polarized; ABEL-JACOBI C → J(C), P ↦ [P − P₀]. WEIL CONJECTURES
    (Weil for curves/abelian varieties, Deligne in general): #C(F_{q^k})
    = q^k + 1 − ∑αᵢᵏ, |αᵢ| = √q, L(T) = ∏(1 − αᵢT) ∈ ℤ[T] of degree 2g,
    #J(F_q) = L(1); for g = 2, #J(F_q) = (N₁² + N₂)/2 − q.
  - FINITENESS THEOREMS: TATE (finite fields) and FALTINGS (number
    fields): Hom(A, B) ⊗ ℤ_ℓ ≅ Hom_{G_K}(T_ℓA, T_ℓB); isogenous ⇔ same
    L-function (same a_p for almost all p). FALTINGS (MORDELL): a curve of
    genus ≥ 2 over a number field has finitely many rational points.
  - MODULAR FORMS OF LEVEL 1: f holomorphic on H with f((aτ + b)/(cτ +
    d)) = (cτ + d)ᵏf(τ), holomorphic at ∞. M_* = C[E₄, E₆]; dim M_k =
    ⌊k/12⌋ (k ≡ 2 mod 12) or ⌊k/12⌋ + 1 (other even k ≥ 0); S_k = ΔM_{k−12}.
    E_k = 1 − (2k/B_k)∑σ_{k−1}(n)qⁿ: E₄ = 1 + 240∑σ₃(n)qⁿ, E₆ = 1 −
    504∑σ₅(n)qⁿ. Δ = q∏(1 − qⁿ)²⁴ = ∑τ(n)qⁿ = (E₄³ − E₆²)/1728, j =
    E₄³/Δ. RAMANUJAN: τ(n) ≡ σ₁₁(n) mod 691.
  - CONGRUENCE SUBGROUPS: Γ₀(N) = {c ≡ 0 mod N}, index μ = N∏_{p|N}(1 +
    1/p); X₀(N) = Γ₀(N)\H* has genus g = 1 + μ/12 − ν₂/4 − ν₃/3 − ν_∞/2,
    and dim S₂(Γ₀(N)) = g. MODULI: Y₀(N) parametrizes (E, C) with C a
    cyclic subgroup of order N. g(X₀(N)) = 0 for N ≤ 10, 12, 13, 16, 18,
    25, and = 1 for N = 11, 14, 15, 17, 19, 20, 21, 24, 27, 32, 36, 49.
  - HECKE OPERATORS: a_m(T_p f) = a_{mp}(f) + p^{k−1}a_{m/p}(f). A
    normalized eigenform has a_{mn} = a_m a_n ((m, n) = 1), a_{p^{r+1}} =
    a_p a_{p^r} − p^{k−1}a_{p^{r−1}} (p ∤ N), a_{p^r} = a_pʳ (p | N).
    NEWFORMS (Atkin-Lehner) satisfy strong multiplicity one; Hecke
    operators are self-adjoint for the PETERSSON inner product (p ∤ N).
  - L(f, s) = ∑aₙn⁻ˢ = ∏(1 − a_p p⁻ˢ + p^{k−1−2s})⁻¹ continues entirely
    and has a functional equation s ↔ k − s. RAMANUJAN-PETERSSON
    (DELIGNE): |a_p| ≤ 2p^{(k−1)/2} for eigenforms; |τ(p)| ≤ 2p^{11/2}.
  - EICHLER-SHIMURA: a weight-2 newform f with coefficient field K_f
    gives an abelian variety A_f ⊆ J₀(N) of dimension [K_f : Q]; J₀(N) ~
    ∏A_f^{m_f}. If K_f = Q, A_f is an elliptic curve with a_p(E) = a_p(f)
    and conductor N. Example: X₀(11) = 11a, f = η(τ)²η(11τ)².
  - ℓ-ADIC REPRESENTATIONS: ρ_{E,ℓ} : G_Q → Aut(T_ℓE) ≅ GL₂(ℤ_ℓ),
    unramified at p ∤ ℓN, char poly of Frob_p = X² − a_pX + p, det =
    cyclotomic character χ_ℓ (Weil pairing), complex conjugation has det
    −1 (ODD). T_ℓ(G_m) = ℤ_ℓ(1). Q(E[n]) ⊇ Q(ζₙ), so E[n] ⊆ E(Q) only
    for n ≤ 2. SERRE: for E without CM, ρ̄_{E,ℓ} is surjective for all
    but finitely many ℓ. MAZUR: rational ℓ-isogenies only for ℓ ∈ {2, 3,
    5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19, 37, 43, 67, 163}.
  - MOD-ℓ TOOLS: division polynomials (ψ₃ = 3x⁴ + 6ax² + 12bx − a² for
    y² = x³ + ax + b); Q(E[2]) = splitting field of the cubic, image of
    ρ̄_{E,2} ⊆ GL₂(F₂) ≅ S₃ is 1, ℤ/2, ℤ/3 or S₃. A newform f of weight
    k gives ρ_{f,λ} with tr Frob_p = a_p, det = χ_ℓ^{k−1} (DELIGNE).
  - ARTIN REPRESENTATIONS AND CHEBOTAREV: the density of p with Frob_p
    in a conjugacy class C is #C/#G. DELIGNE-SERRE: weight-1 newforms ↔
    odd irreducible 2-dimensional Artin representations. Example: the
    S₃-representation of Q(∛2, ζ₃) ↔ η(6τ)η(18τ) (level 108).
  - MODULARITY THEOREM (WILES, TAYLOR-WILES, BREUIL-CONRAD-DIAMOND-
    TAYLOR): every E/Q of conductor N is modular: a_p(E) = a_p(f) for a
    newform f ∈ S₂(Γ₀(N)). FLT: a solution of aᵖ + bᵖ = cᵖ gives the FREY
    CURVE y² = x(x − aᵖ)(x + bᵖ), semistable; RIBET's level lowering puts
    ρ̄_{E,p} in S₂(Γ₀(2)) = 0, contradiction. SERRE'S CONJECTURE (KHARE-
    WINTENBERGER): odd irreducible ρ̄ : G_Q → GL₂(F̄_ℓ) is modular.
  - LANGLANDS PROGRAM: GL₁ = class field theory (Dirichlet characters ↔
    characters of Gal(Q^ab/Q)). Global: n-dimensional ℓ-adic Galois
    representations (geometric) ↔ cuspidal automorphic representations
    of GL_n(A) with matching L-functions. LOCAL LANGLANDS for GL_n
    (HARRIS-TAYLOR, HENNIART). L-GROUPS: ^L GL_n = GL_n(C), SL_n ↔
    PGL_n(C), Sp_{2n} ↔ SO_{2n+1}(C). FUNCTORIALITY: Sym² (GELBART-
    JACQUET), Sym³, Sym⁴ (KIM-SHAHIDI, KIM), all Symⁿ for holomorphic
    forms (NEWTON-THORNE), base change (LANGLANDS, ARTHUR-CLOZEL),
    LANGLANDS-TUNNELL (solvable Artin reps). SATO-TATE (BARNET-LAMB,
    GERAGHTY, HARRIS, TAYLOR): for non-CM E, θ_p (a_p = 2√p cos θ_p) is
    equidistributed for (2/π)sin²θ dθ on [0, π].
  - CM AND TWISTS: y² = x³ − n²x has a_p = 0 for p ≡ 3 mod 4, y² = x³ + k
    has a_p = 0 for p ≡ 2 mod 3; quadratic twist by d multiplies a_p by
    (d/p). Isogenous curves have equal a_p (and conversely, by Faltings).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Explain why j classifies elliptic curves over an algebraically
    closed field but not over Q. (b) For E : y² + y = x³ − x² − 10x − 20
    compute b₂, b₄, b₆, b₈, c₄, c₆, Δ and j, and check 1728Δ = c₄³ − c₆².
    (c) Show y² = x³ + 1 and y² = x³ + 64 are isomorphic over Q but y² =
    x³ + 1 and y² = x³ + 2 are not.
    Answer: (b) b₂ = −4, b₄ = −20, b₆ = −79, b₈ = −21, c₄ = 496, c₆ = 20008,
    Δ = −161051 = −11⁵, j = −122023936/161051. (c) u = 2 works; the
    second pair differ by the twist ratio 2, not a sixth power.
    Teacher explains: (a) Over an algebraically closed field (char ≠ 2,
    3) two curves with the same j can be brought to the same short form
    y² = x³ + ax + b by x ↦ u²x, y ↦ u³y, since (a, b) ↦ (u⁴a, u⁶b) and j
    depends only on a³/b²; there we can extract the needed roots u.
    Over Q we cannot: y² = x³ + b and y² = x³ + b′ are isomorphic over
    Q iff b′/b ∈ Q*⁶. These forms are the TWISTS; for j ≠ 0, 1728 they
    are the quadratic twists dy² = x³ + ax + b. (b) Here a₁ = 0, a₂ =
    −1, a₃ = 1, a₄ = −10, a₆ = −20:
       b₂ = 0 + 4(−1) = −4,  b₄ = 2(−10) + 0 = −20,  b₆ = 1 + 4(−20) = −79,
       b₈ = 0 + 4(−1)(−20) − 0 + (−1)(1) − 100 = 80 − 1 − 100 = −21,
       c₄ = 16 + 480 = 496,  c₆ = 64 + 36(−4)(−20) + 216·79
          = 64 + 2880 + 17064 = 20008,
       Δ = −b₂²b₈ − 8b₄³ − 27b₆² + 9b₂b₄b₆
         = 336 + 64000 − 168507 − 56880 = −161051 = −11⁵.
    Check: c₄³ − c₆² = 122023936 − 400320064 = −278296128 = 1728·(−161051).
    So j = 496³/(−11⁵) = −122023936/161051. The curve has bad reduction
    only at 11; it is the curve 11a, isomorphic to X₀(11). (c) y² = x³ +
    64 with x = 4x′, y = 8y′ gives 64y′² = 64x′³ + 64. Δ changes by u¹² =
    4096: −432·4096 = Δ(x³ + 64) = −16·27·64². For b′/b = 2 we would need
    2 = u⁶, impossible in Q. (Indeed a₇ = −4 for y² = x³ + 1 but a₇ = −1
    for y² = x³ + 2, so they are not even isogenous over Q; over Q(⁶√2)
    they become isomorphic.) Common mistakes: forgetting a₃ in b₆;
    thinking equal j means isomorphic over Q. Now you try: j and Δ of y²
    = x³ − x + 1. (Δ = −368 = −2⁴·23, j = −6912/23)

D2. (a) State Nagell-Lutz and Mazur's theorem, and explain why reduction
    modulo a good prime p ≥ 3 is injective on torsion. (b) Show that P =
    (3, 8) on y² = x³ − 43x + 166 has order 7 and E(Q)_tors ≅ ℤ/7. (c)
    Show that P = (0, 0) on y² + y = x³ − x has infinite order.
    Answer: (b) P, 2P, ..., 6P = (3, 8), (−5, −16), (11, −32), (11, 32),
    (−5, 16), (3, −8), 7P = O. (c) #Ẽ(F₃) = 7, #Ẽ(F₅) = 8 are coprime, so
    the torsion is trivial.
    Teacher explains: (a) NAGELL-LUTZ: on y² = x³ + ax + b, a, b ∈ ℤ, a
    torsion point P ≠ O has integer coordinates and y = 0 or y² divides
    4a³ + 27b². MAZUR: the only torsion groups over Q are ℤ/n, 1 ≤ n ≤
    10, ℤ/12, and ℤ/2 × ℤ/2m, 1 ≤ m ≤ 4 (15 groups). The kernel of E(Q_p)
    → Ẽ(F_p) is the formal group Ê(pℤ_p), which is torsion-free when p
    ≥ 3: it is pro-p, and a point of order p would have valuation ≤
    1/(p − 1) < 1 (ramification e = 1 < p − 1). So E(Q)_tors embeds in
    Ẽ(F_p), and #E(Q)_tors divides every #Ẽ(F_p), p ≥ 3 good.
    (b) Tangent at (3, 8): λ = (3x² − 43)/(2y) = (27 − 43)/16 = −1, so x(2P)
    = λ² − 6 = −5, y(2P) = λ(3 − (−5)) − 8 = −16. Continuing (chords):
    3P = (11, −32), 4P = (11, 32) = −3P. Since 4P = −3P, 7P = O and P ≠ O
    has order 7 (7 is prime). Δ = −2¹⁹·13, so p = 3 is good: a₃ = −3 gives
    #Ẽ(F₃) = 7; p = 5: a₅ = −1, #Ẽ(F₅) = 7. Hence the torsion has order
    dividing 7 and equals ⟨P⟩ ≅ ℤ/7. (c) Δ = 37, so 3 and 5 are good.
    Counting: #Ẽ(F₃) = 3 + 1 − (−3) = 7 and #Ẽ(F₅) = 5 + 1 − (−2) = 8,
    gcd = 1, so E(Q)_tors = 0 and (0, 0) has infinite order: its multiples
    are (0, 0), (1, 0), (−1, −1), (2, −3), (1/4, −5/8), (6, 14), ... and
    never return to O. Common mistakes:
    using p = 2 for full injectivity (on 15a, #Ẽ(F₂) = 4 but the torsion
    ℤ/2 × ℤ/4 has order 8); applying Nagell-Lutz to non-integral models.
    Now you try: torsion of y² = x³ + 1 using p = 5, 7. (gcd(6, 12) = 6 and
    (2, 3) has order 6: ℤ/6)

D3. (a) State Hasse's theorem and derive the zeta function of E/F_q from
    the Frobenius equation. (b) For E : y² = x³ + x + 1 over F₅ list
    E(F₅), find its group structure, and compute #E(F₂₅). (c) For
    y² + y = x³ − x² − 10x − 20 (11a), find #E(F_{2^k}), k = 1, 2, 3, 4.
    Answer: (b) 9 points, E(F₅) ≅ ℤ/9 generated by (0, 1); #E(F₂₅) = 27.
    (c) 5, 5, 5, 25.
    Teacher explains: (a) The q-Frobenius φ is an endomorphism with
    deg(1 − φ) = #E(F_q) (1 − φ is separable). The degree is a positive
    definite quadratic form on End(E), so for a = tr φ, deg(m − nφ) = m²
    − amn + qn² ≥ 0 for all m, n: the discriminant a² − 4q ≤ 0, i.e. |a| ≤
    2√q. φ satisfies φ² − aφ + q = 0; with roots α, β (αβ = q, |α| = √q),
    φᵏ has trace αᵏ + βᵏ, so Nₖ = q^k + 1 − αᵏ − βᵏ and
       Z(E, T) = exp(∑NₖTᵏ/k) = (1 − aT + qT²)/((1 − T)(1 − qT)).
    (b) Squares mod 5 are 0, 1, 4. f(x) = x³ + x + 1: f(0) = 1, f(1) = 3,
    f(2) = 11 ≡ 1, f(3) = 31 ≡ 1, f(4) = 69 ≡ 4. So points (0, ±1),
    (2, ±1), (3, ±1), (4, ±2) and O: 9 points, a = −3. The only groups of
    order 9 are ℤ/9 and (ℤ/3)²; the second needs E[3] ⊆ E(F₅), impossible
    since the Weil pairing forces μ₃ ⊆ F₅*. So E(F₅) ≅ ℤ/9, and (0, 1) has
    order 9 (it is not 3-torsion: 2P = (4, 2) ≠ −P). Over F₂₅: α² + β² =
    a² − 2q = 9 − 10 = −1, so #E(F₂₅) = 25 + 1 + 1 = 27. (c) Here a₂ = −2,
    q = 2: s₁ = −2, sₖ = −2sₖ₋₁ − 2sₖ₋₂ gives s₂ = 4 − 4 = 0, s₃ = 0 + 4 =
    4, s₄ = −8 − 0 = −8. So N₁ = 3 + 2 = 5, N₂ = 5 − 0 = 5, N₃ = 9 − 4 = 5,
    N₄ = 17 + 8 = 25. (Note a₂ ≡ 0 mod 2: 11a is SUPERSINGULAR at 2.)
    Common mistakes: forgetting O; using N₂ = N₁²; claiming (ℤ/3)² ⊆
    E(F₅). Now you try: y² = x³ − x over F₇: a₇ = 0, so #E(F₇) = 8 and
    #E(F₄₉) = 49 + 1 + 14 = 64. (8, 64; E(F₇) ≅ ℤ/2 × ℤ/4)

D4. (a) Define supersingular curves and state Deuring's count. (b) Show
    y² = x³ − x is supersingular exactly at p ≡ 3 mod 4 and y² = x³ + 1 at
    p ≡ 2 mod 3. (c) Find all supersingular j over F̄₁₃, F̄₁₉, F̄₂₃ and
    compare with g(X₀(p)) + 1.
    Answer: (c) p = 13: j = 5; p = 19: j = 7, 18 (≡ 1728); p = 23: j = 0,
    3, 19. Counts 1, 2, 3 = g(X₀(p)) + 1 with g = 0, 1, 2.
    Teacher explains: (a) E/F̄_p is SUPERSINGULAR if E[p](F̄_p) = 0,
    equivalently a_p ≡ 0 mod p for E/F_p, equivalently End(E) is an order
    in the quaternion algebra over Q ramified at p and ∞. DEURING: the
    number of supersingular j is ⌊p/12⌋ + ε, ε = 0, 1, 1, 2 for p ≡ 1, 5,
    7, 11 mod 12; all lie in F_{p²}. (b) For p ≡ 3 mod 4, x ↦ −x
    permutes F_p and f(−x) = −f(x) with −1 a non-square: exactly one of
    f(x), f(−x) is a nonzero square when f(x) ≠ 0, so the affine count is
    2·(p − 3)/2 + 3 = p and #E = p + 1: a_p = 0. For p ≡ 1 mod 4 the curve
    has CM by ℤ[i] with i split; one checks a_p = ±2a where p = a² + b²,
    a odd (p = 5: −2, 13: 6, 17: 2, 29: −10), never 0. Similarly for y² =
    x³ + 1: when p ≡ 2 mod 3, x ↦ x³ is a bijection of F_p, so for each
    y there is exactly one x with x³ = y² − 1: p affine points, a_p = 0.
    For p ≡ 1 mod 3, 4p = a_p² + 3b² with a_p ≠ 0 (p = 7: 28 = 16 + 12,
    a₇ = −4). (c) Using
    the Legendre form y² = x(x − 1)(x − λ): supersingular iff Hₚ(λ) =
    ∑_{i≤m} C(m, i)²λⁱ = 0, m = (p − 1)/2, and j = 256(λ² − λ + 1)³/(λ²(λ −
    1)²). For p = 13 the roots give only j = 5: ⌊13/12⌋ + 0 = 1. For p =
    19: j = 7 and 18 = 1728 mod 19 (19 ≡ 3 mod 4): ⌊19/12⌋ + 1 = 2. For
    p = 23: j = 0, 1728 ≡ 3 and 19: ⌊23/12⌋ + 2 = 3. Genus of X₀(p): 0,
    1, 2 respectively — the supersingular points of X₀(p) mod p count
    g + 1. Common mistakes: forgetting j = 0 and 1728 counts; calling E
    ordinary because a_p ≠ 0 (for p ≤ 3, a_p ≡ 0 mod p suffices: a₂ = −2
    for 11a is supersingular). Now you try: p = 37. (3 values, j = 8
    and a conjugate pair in F₁₃₆₉ ∖ F₃₇)

D5. (a) Classify reduction types and define the conductor. (b) Determine
    the reduction of E : y² = x³ + x² + p and E′ : y² = x³ − x² + p at p ≥
    5 and verify a_p by counting for p = 5, 7. (c) Explain Néron-Ogg-
    Shafarevich and why 11a has potentially multiplicative reduction at 11.
    Answer: (b) Δ(E) = −16p(4 + 27p), Δ(E′) = 16p(4 − 27p): both have v_p
    = 1, multiplicative. E is split (a_p = 1); E′ is split iff p ≡ 1
    mod 4: a₅ = 1, a₇ = −1. (c) v₁₁(j) = −5 < 0.
    Teacher explains: (a) Take a minimal model. Mod p the cubic curve is
    smooth (GOOD), has a node (MULTIPLICATIVE) or a cusp (ADDITIVE). The
    smooth locus E_ns(F_p) is a group: F_p* (split node), the norm-one
    torus of F_{p²} (non-split, p + 1 elements) or F_p (cusp). Setting
    a_p = p − #E_ns(F_p) gives 1, −1, 0. Conductor exponent f_p = 0, 1, 2
    for p ≥ 5 (wild part only at 2, 3). (b) For E: b₂ = 4, b₄ = 0, b₆ = b₈
    = 4p, so Δ = −16·4p − 27·16p² = −16p(4 + 27p), v_p(Δ) = 1: the model
    is minimal and the reduction is multiplicative. Mod p, y² = x²(x + 1)
    has a node at (0, 0) with tangents y = ±x: slopes in F_p, SPLIT, a_p =
    1. Count for p = 5: #Ẽ(F₅) including the node is 5 = p, so a₅ = p + 1 −
    5 = 1. ✓. For E′: y² = x²(x − 1) has tangents y² = −x², rational iff
    −1 is a square: p ≡ 1 mod 4. p = 5: split, a₅ = 1 (count 5); p = 7:
    non-split, count 9 = p + 2, a₇ = 8 − 9 = −1 ✓. (c) For ℓ ≠ p, E has
    good reduction at p iff the inertia group I_p acts trivially on T_ℓE
    (Néron-Ogg-Shafarevich). If v_p(j) < 0 then over a quadratic
    extension E ≅ G_m/q^ℤ (TATE CURVE) with v(q) = −v(j) > 0; the inertia
    acts through ( 1 * ; 0 1 ) nontrivially, so there is no good reduction
    over any extension. 11a: j = −122023936/11⁵, v₁₁(j) = −5; the Tate
    period has v(q) = 5 and the Tamagawa number is c₁₁ = 5; a₁₁ = 1
    (split). Common mistakes: using a non-minimal model (y² = x³ + p⁶ is
    y² = x³ + 1 in disguise); calling a cusp "split". Now you try: type of
    y² = x³ + 5 at 5. (additive: cusp y² = x³, a₅ = 0, f₅ = 2)

D6. (a) Describe descent via a 2-isogeny. (b) Prove y² = x³ − x and
    y² = x³ + x have rank 0, and deduce that 1 is not a congruent number.
    (c) Show 5 is congruent: find the point on y² = x³ − 25x and the
    triangle.
    Answer: (b) #α·#α′ = 2·2 and 1·4, so 2^r = 1. (c) P = (−4, 6), 2P =
    (1681/144, −62279/1728); triangle (3/2, 20/3, 41/6).
    Teacher explains: (a) For E : y² = x³ + ax² + bx and E′ : y² = x³ −
    2ax² + (a² − 4b)x there are 2-isogenies φ : E → E′, φ′ : E′ → E, and
    homomorphisms α : E(Q) → Q*/Q*², (x, y) ↦ x, (0, 0) ↦ b, O ↦ 1, with
    kernel φ′(E′(Q)); similarly α′. Then 2^r = #α(E(Q))·#α′(E′(Q))/4.
    A squarefree d | b lies in the image iff N² = dM⁴ + aM²e² + (b/d)e⁴
    has an integer solution with M ≠ 0 (x = dM²/e²). (b) E : y² = x³ − x:
    a = 0, b = −1, E′ : y² = x³ + 4x. α(E): d ∈ {±1}, and −1 = α(0, 0) is
    attained: #α = 2. α′(E′): b′ = 4, d ∈ {±1, ±2}; d < 0 gives N² < 0,
    impossible; d = 2: N² = 2M⁴ + 2e⁴ has M = e = 1, N = 2 (the point (2,
    4)). So #α′ = 2 and 2^r = 4/4 = 1: r = 0. For y² = x³ + x (b = 1), E′:
    y² = x³ − 4x: α = {1} (d = −1: N² = −M⁴ − e⁴ < 0); α′ ∋ −4 ≡ −1 (image
    of (0, 0)) and 2 (N² = 2M⁴ − 2e⁴, M = e = 1: the point (2, 0)), so α′
    = {±1, ±2}, 2^r = 4/4 = 1. n is congruent iff y² = x³ − n²x has a
    point with y ≠ 0; the torsion is E[2] (four points with y = 0), so n
    congruent ⇔ r ≥ 1. With r = 0 for n = 1: no rational right triangle
    has area 1 (Fermat). (c) (−4)³ + 100 = 36 = 6², so P = (−4, 6); it has
    infinite order since 2P = (1681/144, ...) is non-integral. From 2P:
    x = (c/2)² gives c/2 = 41/12, c = 41/6; the triangle with legs 3/2,
    20/3 has area 5 and (3/2)² + (20/3)² = 1681/36 = (41/6)². Common
    mistakes: forgetting to include the images of the 2-torsion points;
    dividing by 4 twice. Now you try: 6 is congruent — point on y² = x³ −
    36x. ((−3, 9), from the triangle 3, 4, 5; also (12, 36))

D7. (a) State BSD and what is proven. (b) Check the BSD formula for 11a:
    r = 0, E(Q)_tors ≅ ℤ/5, c₁₁ = 5, Ш = 0, Ω = 1.26920930..., L(E, 1) =
    0.25384186.... (c) For 37a (y² + y = x³ − x), L′(E, 1) = 0.30599977...,
    Ω = 5.98691729..., ĥ(P) = 0.05111140... for P = (0, 0): check BSD and
    explain the sign of the functional equation.
    Answer: (b) L/Ω = 0.2 = 1·5/5². (c) Ω·Reg = 0.306000 = L′(E, 1) (with
    c₃₇ = 1, #tors = 1, Ш = 0); w = −1 = a₃₇.
    Teacher explains: (a) BSD: ord_{s=1}L(E, s) = r = rank E(Q), and
       L^{(r)}(E, 1)/r! = Ω·Reg(E/Q)·#Ш(E/Q)·∏_p c_p / #E(Q)_tors².
    Proven: if ord ≤ 1 then r = ord and Ш is finite (Gross-Zagier gives a
    Heegner point of infinite order when L′(E, 1) ≠ 0; Kolyvagin's Euler
    system bounds the Selmer group); the leading-term formula is known in
    many such cases. In general open (Clay problem). (b) Δ = −11⁵ < 0, so
    E(R) is connected and Ω is the real period. c₁₁: split multiplicative
    with v(Δ) = 5 gives c₁₁ = 5. RHS = Ω·1·1·5/25 = Ω/5 = 0.25384186 =
    L(E, 1) ✓. (c) Δ = 37, c₃₇ = v(Δ) = 1, torsion trivial (D2), rank 1
    with generator (0, 0) (so Reg = ĥ(P)), Ш = 0. RHS = 5.98691729 ×
    0.05111140 = 0.30600. ✓ The root number: for prime conductor N, w =
    a_N (w = −∏_p w_p, w_p = −a_p at multiplicative p). a₃₇ = −1 (non-
    split) gives w = −1, so L(E, 1) = 0 is forced and r is odd (parity
    conjecture, known here). For 11a, a₁₁ = 1, w = +1; for 389a (rank 2)
    a₃₈₉ = 1; for 5077a (rank 3) w = −1. Common mistakes: forgetting the
    square of the torsion order; using the connected-component period
    when E(R) has two components (Δ > 0). Now you try: sign of L(E, s)
    for 19a (a₁₉ = 1) and its expected parity. (w = +1, even rank; r = 0)

D8. (a) Define abelian varieties, polarizations and Jacobians; state
    Poincaré reducibility and the Weil bounds. (b) For C : y² = x⁵ + x² +
    1 over F₃ (genus 2) one counts N₁ = #C(F₃) = 6, N₂ = #C(F₉) = 18. Find
    L(T), #J(F₃), and check the Weil bounds. (c) Why is not every
    2-dimensional complex torus an abelian variety?
    Answer: (b) L(T) = 1 + 2T + 6T² + 6T³ + 9T⁴, #J(F₃) = 24. (c) It needs
    a Riemann form; a generic lattice in C² admits none.
    Teacher explains: (a) An ABELIAN VARIETY is a complete connected group
    variety; rigidity makes it commutative, and it is projective. A
    POLARIZATION is an isogeny λ : A → A^∨ of the form φ_L for an ample
    L; principal if an isomorphism (Jacobians are principally polarized
    by the theta divisor). POINCARÉ: every abelian subvariety B ⊆ A has a
    complement up to finite intersection, so A ~ ∏Aᵢ^{nᵢ} with Aᵢ simple
    and End⁰(A) = ∏M_{nᵢ}(Dᵢ), Dᵢ division algebras. WEIL: over F_q the
    Frobenius eigenvalues αᵢ (2g of them) have |αᵢ| = √q, so |N₁ − (q +
    1)| ≤ 2g√q and (√q − 1)^{2g} ≤ #A(F_q) ≤ (√q + 1)^{2g}.
    (b) Write L(T) = 1 + c₁T + c₂T² + qc₁T³ + q²T⁴ with sₖ = ∑αᵢᵏ = qᵏ + 1
    − Nₖ: s₁ = 4 − 6 = −2, s₂ = 10 − 18 = −8. Then c₁ = −s₁ = 2 and c₂ =
    (s₁² − s₂)/2 = (4 + 8)/2 = 6. So L(T) = 1 + 2T + 6T² + 6T³ + 9T⁴ and
    #J(F₃) = L(1) = 1 + 2 + 6 + 6 + 9 = 24. Check with the formula (N₁² +
    N₂)/2 − q = (36 + 18)/2 − 3 = 24 ✓. Weil: |6 − 4| = 2 ≤ 4√3 ≈ 6.93,
    and (√3 − 1)⁴ ≈ 0.29 ≤ 24 ≤ (√3 + 1)⁴ ≈ 55.7 ✓. (The curve is smooth
    at 3: disc(x⁵ + x² + 1) = 53·61.) (c) A = C²/Λ is algebraic iff there
    is a positive-definite Hermitian H with E = Im H integral on Λ
    (Riemann relations). For a period matrix (I | Z), Z ∈ M₂(C), this
    imposes Z symmetric with Im Z > 0 after a change of basis: the
    abelian surfaces form a 3-dimensional family, all tori a
    4-dimensional one. Common mistakes: forgetting the symmetry c₃ = qc₁;
    using N₁ alone to find L(T) when g = 2. Now you try: y² = x⁵ − x over
    F₅ with N₁ = 6, N₂ = 6. (L = 1 − 10T² + 25T⁴ = (1 − 5T²)², #J = 16)

D9. (a) Prove M_*(SL₂(ℤ)) is generated by E₄, E₆ using the valence
    formula, and give dim M_k. (b) Check E₄² = E₈ to order q² and
    compute j = E₄³/Δ to order q. (c) Using Hecke multiplicativity find
    τ(4), τ(6), τ(8), τ(9), τ(16) from τ(2) = −24, τ(3) = 252, and verify
    Ramanujan's congruence mod 691 and Deligne's bound at p = 2, 3.
    Answer: (a) dim M_k = ⌊k/12⌋ (+1 unless k ≡ 2 mod 12). (b) 61920 both;
    j = q⁻¹ + 744 + 196884q + .... (c) −1472, −6048, 84480, −113643,
    987136.
    Teacher explains: (a) VALENCE FORMULA: for 0 ≠ f ∈ M_k, v_∞(f) +
    v_i(f)/2 + v_ρ(f)/3 + ∑_{other p} v_p(f) = k/12. Hence M_k = 0 for k
    < 0 and k = 2; M_4 = CE₄ (zero only at ρ), M_6 = CE₆ (zero only at
    i). Δ = (E₄³ − E₆²)/1728 has v_∞ = 1 and no other zeros, so f ↦ fΔ
    is an isomorphism M_{k−12} → S_k, and M_k = CE_k ⊕ ΔM_{k−12}; by
    induction M_k is spanned by E₄ᵃE₆ᵇ, 4a + 6b = k. The formula
    follows: dim M₁₂ = 2, M₁₄ = 1, M₂₄ = 3, M₂₆ = 2.
    (b) E₄ = 1 + 240q + 2160q² + ..., so E₄² = 1 + 480q + (2·2160 +
    240²)q² = 1 + 480q + 61920q²; E₈ = 1 + 480∑σ₇(n)qⁿ has 480σ₇(2) =
    480·129 = 61920 ✓ (dim M₈ = 1 forces equality). For j: E₄³ = 1 + 720q
    + (3·2160 + 3·240²)q² = 1 + 720q + 179280q², and 1/Δ = q⁻¹(1 − 24q +
    252q²)⁻¹ = q⁻¹(1 + 24q + 324q² + ...). Product: q⁻¹ + (720 + 24) +
    (179280 + 720·24 + 324)q = q⁻¹ + 744 + 196884q.
    (c) Weight 12: τ(mn) = τ(m)τ(n) for coprime m, n and τ(p^{r+1}) =
    τ(p)τ(pʳ) − p¹¹τ(p^{r−1}). 2¹¹ = 2048, 3¹¹ = 177147.
       τ(4) = 576 − 2048 = −1472,    τ(6) = (−24)(252) = −6048,
       τ(8) = (−24)(−1472) − 2048(−24) = 35328 + 49152 = 84480,
       τ(9) = 63504 − 177147 = −113643,
       τ(16) = (−24)(84480) − 2048(−1472) = −2027520 + 3014656 = 987136.
    691: σ₁₁(2) = 2049 ≡ 2049 − 1382 = 667 ≡ −24 ✓. Deligne: |τ(2)| = 24
    ≤ 2·2^{5.5} ≈ 90.5 and 252 ≤ 2·3^{5.5} ≈ 841.8 ✓. Common mistakes:
    using p^{k−1} = p instead of p¹¹; multiplying τ(2)τ(4) for τ(8)
    (2 and 4 are not coprime). Now you try: τ(25) and τ(10) from τ(5) =
    4830. (23328900 − 48828125 = −25499225; −115920)

D10. (a) Compute g(X₀(N)) for N = 11, 23, 37 and interpret dim S₂(Γ₀(N)).
     (b) Show f = η(τ)²η(11τ)² = q − 2q² − q³ + 2q⁴ + q⁵ + 2q⁶ − 2q⁷ − 2q⁹
     − 2q¹⁰ + q¹¹ + ... is a Hecke eigenform whose a_p match 11a for p =
     2, 3, 5, 7, 13, and check a₄, a₈, a₉, a₂₅ with the recursion. (c)
     Explain the congruences a_p ≡ 1 + p mod 5 and a_p ≡ τ(p) mod 11.
     Answer: (a) g = 1, 2, 2; μ = 12, 24, 38; cusps 2. (b) a_p: −2, −1, 1,
     −2, 4 both; a₄ = 2, a₈ = 0, a₉ = −2, a₂₅ = −4. (c) Eisenstein
     congruence (5-torsion) and Δ ≡ f mod 11.
     Teacher explains: (a) For prime p, μ = p + 1, ν_∞ = 2, ν₂ = 1 +
     (−1/p), ν₃ = 1 + (−3/p). N = 11: ν₂ = 1 + (−1) = 0, ν₃ = 1 + (−1) = 0
     (11 ≡ 2 mod 3), g = 1 + 1 − 0 − 0 − 1 = 1. N = 23: μ = 24, ν₂ = 0,
     ν₃ = 0, g = 1 + 2 − 1 = 2. N = 37: μ = 38, ν₂ = 2, ν₃ = 2, g = 1 +
     38/12 − 1/2 − 2/3 − 1 = 2. Since dim S₂(Γ₀(N)) = g (holomorphic
     differentials f(τ)dτ on X₀(N)): one form at 11, two at 23 (a
     Galois-conjugate pair with coefficients in Q(√5), giving the simple
     abelian surface J₀(23)), two at 37 (two rational newforms: 37a and
     37b). (b) η(τ)²η(11τ)² is a weight-2 cusp form on Γ₀(11) (∑ of
     exponents × levels: 2·1 + 2·11 = 24 ≡ 0 mod 24, weight (2 + 2)/2 = 2),
     and dim S₂ = 1, so it is automatically a Hecke eigenform. Points on
     11a: a₂ = 3 − 5 = −2, a₃ = −1, a₅ = 1, a₇ = −2, a₁₃ = 4: they agree
     (EICHLER-SHIMURA: T_p = Frob + Ver on J₀(11) = X₀(11) mod p). The
     recursion a_{p^{r+1}} = a_p a_{pʳ} − p a_{p^{r−1}}:
        a₄ = 4 − 2 = 2,  a₈ = (−2)(2) − 2(−2) = 0,  a₉ = 1 − 3 = −2,
        a₂₅ = 1 − 5 = −4,  a₆ = a₂a₃ = 2,  a₁₂₁ = a₁₁² = 1.
     (c) E(Q) has a point of order 5, so E[5] ⊇ ℤ/5 (trivial action) and
     the quotient is μ₅ (Weil pairing): the Frobenius has eigenvalues 1
     and p mod 5, so a_p ≡ 1 + p: a₂ = −2 ≡ 3, a₃ = −1 ≡ 4, a₇ = −2 ≡ 8 ✓.
     This is a congruence between f and the Eisenstein series of weight
     2, level 11. Mod 11: ∏(1 − qⁿ)¹¹ ≡ ∏(1 − q¹¹ⁿ) (Frobenius on
     q-series), so η(τ)¹¹ ≡ η(11τ) and Δ = η²·(η¹¹)² ≡ η(τ)²η(11τ)² mod 11:
     τ(2) = −24 ≡ −2, τ(3) = 252 ≡ −1, τ(5) = 4830 ≡ 1 mod 11 ✓. Common
     mistakes: counting only the cusp ∞; using a_{p²} = a_p² for good p.
     Now you try: g(X₀(N)) for N = 14 and N = 22. (μ = 24, 4 cusps, g =
     1; μ = 36, 4 cusps, g = 2 = two copies of the 11-form: J₀(22) ~ 11a²)

D11. (a) Construct ρ_{E,ℓ} and list its properties (unramified primes,
     traces, determinant, oddness). (b) For E = 11a write the char poly
     of Frob₂ and factor it mod 5. (c) Determine Q(E[2]) and the image of
     ρ̄_{E,2} for y² = x³ − x, y² = x³ + 1, y² = x³ − 3x + 1, y² = x³ − 2.
     (d) Find Q(E[3]) for y² = x³ + 1.
     Answer: (b) X² + 2X + 2 ≡ (X − 1)(X − 2) mod 5. (c) Q, Q(√−3), cubic
     cyclic, Q(∛2, √−3); images 1, ℤ/2, ℤ/3, S₃. (d) Q(∛2, √−3), degree 6.
     Teacher explains: (a) G_Q acts on E[ℓⁿ] ≅ (ℤ/ℓⁿ)² compatibly; the
     inverse limit T_ℓE ≅ ℤ_ℓ² gives ρ_{E,ℓ} : G_Q → GL₂(ℤ_ℓ). For p ∤ ℓN,
     E has good reduction and reduction identifies E[ℓⁿ] with Ẽ[ℓⁿ]
     (Néron-Ogg-Shafarevich), so ρ is unramified at p and Frob_p acts as
     the Frobenius endomorphism: char poly X² − a_pX + p. The Weil
     pairing identifies ∧²T_ℓE ≅ ℤ_ℓ(1), so det ρ = χ_ℓ (cyclotomic);
     complex conjugation c has χ_ℓ(c) = −1, so det ρ(c) = −1 — ρ is ODD.
     (b) a₂ = −2: X² + 2X + 2. Mod 5: X² + 2X + 2 ≡ X² − 3X + 2 = (X − 1)(X
     − 2); the eigenvalues are 1 and 2 = p. ρ̄_{E,5} is reducible,
     semisimplification 1 ⊕ χ₅, as the rational 5-torsion point predicts.
     (c) Q(E[2]) is the splitting field of the cubic. x³ − x = x(x − 1)(x
     + 1): trivial image. x³ + 1 = (x + 1)(x² − x + 1): Q(√−3), image ℤ/2.
     x³ − 3x + 1: irreducible (no roots ±1) with discriminant −4(−27) −
     27 = 81 = 9², so Galois group A₃ ≅ ℤ/3. x³ − 2: discriminant −108,
     not a square: S₃ ≅ GL₂(F₂), ρ̄ surjective. (d) ψ₃ = 3x⁴ + 12x = 3x(x³
     + 4). x = 0 gives (0, ±1) ∈ E(Q) (order 3). x³ = −4 gives y² = −3.
     So Q(E[3]) = Q(∛4, √−3, ζ₃) = Q(∛2, √−3), of degree 6, containing
     Q(ζ₃) as the Weil pairing demands; the image is a subgroup of order
     6 of a Borel subgroup of GL₂(F₃) (order 48); it fixes (0, 1). Common
     mistakes: taking det ρ = 1 (it is χ_ℓ, never trivial over Q);
     forgetting that Q(E[n]) ⊇ Q(ζₙ). Now you try: image of ρ̄_{E,2}
     for 11a, whose 2-division cubic 4x³ − 4x² − 40x − 79 is irreducible
     of discriminant −2⁴·11⁵. (S₃: −11⁵ is not a square)

D12. (a) Explain the Langlands correspondence for GL₁ and GL₂ over Q
     and the role of L-functions. (b) Let K = Q(∛2, ζ₃) and ρ : Gal(K/Q)
     ≅ S₃ → GL₂(C) the 2-dimensional irreducible representation. Compute
     tr ρ(Frob_p) by cases and the Chebotarev densities; compare with
     g = η(6τ)η(18τ) = q − q⁷ − q¹³ − q¹⁹ + 2q³¹ − q³⁷ + 2q⁴³ + ... . (c)
     Outline the proof of Fermat's Last Theorem.
     Answer: (b) tr = 2 (x³ − 2 splits mod p), −1 (irreducible), 0 (one
     root); densities 1/6, 1/3, 1/2; matches g (weight 1, level 108).
     Teacher explains: (a) GL₁: continuous characters of G_Q with finite
     image are Dirichlet characters χ (Kronecker-Weber: Q^ab = ⋃Q(ζₙ)),
     and L(s, χ) is both an Artin and a Hecke L-function — this is class
     field theory, the automorphic side being Hecke characters of
     A_Q*/Q*. GL₂: to a cuspidal newform f of weight k ≥ 1 corresponds a
     Galois representation ρ_f (Eichler-Shimura k = 2, Deligne k ≥ 2,
     Deligne-Serre k = 1) with L(s, ρ_f) = L(s, f): trace of Frobenius =
     Hecke eigenvalue. The converse (every odd, geometric ρ comes from a
     form) is modularity. L-functions are the bridge: equal Euler factors
     at almost all p pin down both objects (strong multiplicity one,
     Chebotarev). (b) For p ∤ 6: if p ≡ 2 mod 3, x ↦ x³ is bijective on F_p,
     so x³ − 2 has exactly one root and Frob_p is a transposition: tr = 0.
     If p ≡ 1 mod 3, Frob_p ∈ A₃: identity (x³ − 2 splits, tr = 2) or a
     3-cycle (irreducible, tr = −1). In S₃ the classes have sizes 1, 2, 3,
     so the densities are 1/6, 2/6, 3/6. Check with g: p = 7, 13, 19, 37
     (x³ − 2 has no roots) give −1; p = 31, 43 (roots exist; 31 = 2² +
     27·1², 43 = 4² + 27·1²) give 2; p = 5, 11, 17, ... give 0. Among the
     20 primes 5 ≤ p ≤ 79: 2 split, 8 inert, 10 with one root — densities
     are only approached slowly. g is a weight-1 newform of level 108 =
     Artin conductor of ρ, confirming Artin's conjecture here (known for
     all odd 2-dimensional ρ). (c) Suppose aᵖ + bᵖ = cᵖ, p ≥ 5, abc ≠ 0
     coprime, b even, a ≡ −1 mod 4. The FREY CURVE E : y² = x(x − aᵖ)(x +
     bᵖ) has Δ = 16(abc)^{2p}, is semistable with conductor rad(abc).
     ρ̄_{E,p} is irreducible (Mazur) and unramified away from 2p, finite
     flat at p. WILES (with Taylor-Wiles): semistable E is modular, so
     ρ̄_{E,p} comes from S₂(Γ₀(N)). RIBET's level lowering strips all odd
     primes: ρ̄_{E,p} comes from S₂(Γ₀(2)) = 0 (g(X₀(2)) = 0).
     Contradiction. Common mistakes: thinking Wiles proved all of
     modularity (the full theorem is BCDT 2001); forgetting Frobenius
     trace 0 for transpositions. Now you try: the density of primes with
     a_p = 0 for y² = x³ + 1 (CM by Q(√−3)). (1/2: all p ≡ 2 mod 3, and
     no p ≡ 1 mod 3, since 4p = a_p² + 3b² forces a_p ≠ 0)
