M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) Every closed orientable 3-manifold is integral surgery on a link
        in S³.
     b) The Alexander polynomial detects the unknot.
     c) Khovanov homology detects the unknot.
     d) If det K is a perfect square, K is slice.
     e) A knot with Δ_K = 1 is topologically slice.
     f) Lens spaces with isomorphic fundamental groups are homeomorphic.
     g) Homotopy equivalent lens spaces are homeomorphic.
     h) A closed simply connected 3-manifold is homeomorphic to S³.
     i) Every closed simply connected topological 4-manifold is
        smoothable.
     j) Smooth closed simply connected 4-manifolds with isomorphic
        intersection forms are diffeomorphic.
     k) An even unimodular form has signature ≡ 0 (mod 8).
     l) An even unimodular form of a closed topological 4-manifold has
        signature ≡ 0 (mod 16).
     m) The figure-eight knot is slice.
     n) span V(K) equals the crossing number of K for every knot.
     o) The Heegaard genus of a closed 3-manifold equals the rank of π₁.
     p) A hyperbolic knot has only finitely many non-hyperbolic
        surgeries.
     q) The hyperbolic volume of a knot complement is a knot invariant.
     r) If Δ_K is monic, K is fibered.
     s) SW(X₁ # X₂) = 0 whenever b⁺(X₁), b⁺(X₂) ≥ 1.
     t) Every lens space is an L-space.
     u) τ(K) = −σ(K)/2 for every knot K.
     v) Every homology 3-sphere bounds a contractible topological
        4-manifold.
     w) Every homology 3-sphere bounds a contractible smooth 4-manifold.
     x) Every Anosov element of SL(2, Z) has an irrational stretch
        factor.
     Answers:
       a) T (Lickorish-Wallace)
       b) F: the Kinoshita-Terasaka knot (11 crossings) has Δ = 1
       c) T (Kronheimer-Mrowka)
       d) F: the granny knot, det 9, σ = −4
       e) T (Freedman)
       f) F: L(5,1) and L(5,2)         g) F: L(7,1) and L(7,2)
       h) T (Perelman)                 i) F: the E₈-manifold
       j) F: X₅ and 9CP² # 44CP̄²       k) T
       l) F: the E₈-manifold (σ = 8); mod 16 needs smooth + spin
       m) F: det 5 is not a square     n) F: T(3,4), span 5, c = 8
       o) F: some Seifert fibred spaces with rank 2 have genus 3
          (Boileau-Zieschang)
       p) T (Thurston)                 q) T (Mostow rigidity)
       r) F: Kinoshita-Terasaka (Δ = 1 but g = 2 ≠ 0 = deg Δ/2)
       s) T                            t) T
       u) F: T(4,5) (−σ/2 = 4, τ = 6)  v) T (Freedman)
       w) F: Σ(2,3,5) (Rokhlin μ = 1)
       x) T (tr² − 4 is never a square for |tr| ≥ 3)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: work with three running examples — the trefoil, the
  figure-eight and the Poincaré sphere — and compute EVERY invariant on
  them before meeting new ones: colourings (9 and 3), Δ (t − 1 + t⁻¹ and
  −t + 3 − t⁻¹), det (3 and 5), σ (−2 and 0), V (chiral versus
  symmetric), HFK̂ (ranks 1, 1, 1 and 1, 3, 1), monodromy (periodic
  versus Anosov), geometry (Seifert versus hyperbolic), and surgery (±1
  on the trefoil gives Σ(2,3,5) and Σ(2,3,7)). Insist on a table of sign
  conventions (which trefoil is "right", σ(RHT) = −2, τ(RHT) = 1,
  S³_{−p/q}(O) = L(p, q)) and on sanity checks: Δ(1) = ±1, V(1) = 1,
  |V(−1)| = |Δ(−1)| = det, |H₁(S³_{p/q}(K))| = |p|, χ = 2 + b₂, b⁺ + b⁻ =
  b₂. For 4-manifolds, always classify the form first (rank, σ, type,
  definite?) and only then ask which theorem (Freedman, Rokhlin,
  Donaldson, Furuta, SW) is relevant. Harder: prove that Seifert's
  algorithm gives minimal genus for alternating diagrams; prove the
  Kauffman-Murasugi-Thistlethwaite span theorem; compute HFK̂ of
  T(3,4) from the staircase and HF⁺ of S³_p(K) via the mapping cone
  formula; prove Rokhlin's theorem from the Â-genus and the quaternionic
  structure of the spinor bundle; derive the Thom conjecture from the
  adjunction inequality via blow-ups; show that the figure-eight complement
  is the union of two ideal tetrahedra and compute its cusp shape; and
  construct an exotic R⁴ from Donaldson's theorem plus Freedman's work.


================================================================================
TOPIC 13.6 — QUANTUM TOPOLOGY AND TOPOLOGICAL QUANTUM FIELD THEORY: JONES
POLYNOMIAL, QUANTUM GROUPS, RESHETIKHIN-TURAEV, ATIYAH AXIOMS, DIJKGRAAF-
WITTEN, TURAEV-VIRO, KHOVANOV HOMOLOGY, VERLINDE FORMULA
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - LINK DIAGRAMS: two diagrams represent isotopic links iff they differ by
    planar isotopy and REIDEMEISTER moves R1 (kink), R2 (two strands), R3
    (triangle). WRITHE w(D) = ∑ (signs of crossings), invariant under R2,
    R3, changed by ±1 by R1; for a 2-component link lk = (∑ inter-component
    signs)/2. Invariants of R2 + R3 only are invariants of FRAMED links.
  - KAUFFMAN BRACKET: ⟨O⟩ = 1, ⟨D ⊔ O⟩ = d⟨D⟩ with d = −A² − A⁻², and
    ⟨crossing⟩ = A⟨A-smoothing⟩ + A⁻¹⟨B-smoothing⟩. STATE SUM: ⟨D⟩ =
    ∑_s A^{a(s)−b(s)} d^{|s|−1} over the 2ⁿ states. Invariant under R2, R3;
    R1 multiplies by −A^{±3}. X(D) = (−A³)^{−w(D)}⟨D⟩ is an isotopy invariant
    of oriented links. For a positive crossing the A-smoothing is the
    oriented smoothing.
  - JONES POLYNOMIAL: V_L(t) = X(D) at A = t^{−1/4}. V(O) = 1, V(unlink of
    c components) = (−t^{1/2} − t^{−1/2})^{c−1}. SKEIN RELATION t⁻¹V(L₊) −
    tV(L₋) = (t^{1/2} − t^{−1/2})V(L₀). V(right trefoil) = t + t³ − t⁴,
    V(4₁) = t⁻² − t⁻¹ + 1 − t + t², V(H₊) = −t^{1/2} − t^{5/2}, V(T(p, q)) =
    t^{(p−1)(q−1)/2}(1 − t^{p+1} − t^{q+1} + t^{p+q})/(1 − t²).
  - PROPERTIES: V(K̄)(t) = V(K)(t⁻¹) (mirror); V(K₁ # K₂) = V(K₁)V(K₂);
    V(L₁ ⊔ L₂) = −(t^{1/2} + t^{−1/2})V(L₁)V(L₂); knots have V ∈ Z[t^{±1}]
    and V(1) = 1, V′(1) = 0; V_L(1) = (−2)^{c−1}; |V_K(−1)| = det K =
    |Δ_K(−1)|; V_K(e^{2πi/3}) = 1; V_L(i) = (−√2)^{c−1}(−1)^{Arf} or 0.
    Mutants share V. Reversing one component K of L multiplies V by
    t^{−3 lk(K, L∖K)}.
  - KAUFFMAN-MURASUGI-THISTLETHWAITE: span⟨D⟩ ≤ 4n for a connected
    n-crossing diagram, with equality for REDUCED ALTERNATING diagrams, so
    span V = n = crossing number (Tait's conjecture). Non-alternating prime
    knots have span V < c(K) (8₁₉ = T(3, 4): V = t³ + t⁵ − t⁸, span 5 < 8).
  - BRAIDS: Artin's B_n = ⟨σ₁, ..., σ_{n−1} | σᵢσⱼ = σⱼσᵢ (|i − j| ≥ 2),
    σᵢσᵢ₊₁σᵢ = σᵢ₊₁σᵢσᵢ₊₁⟩. ALEXANDER: every link is a closed braid β̂.
    MARKOV: β̂ ≅ γ̂ iff related by conjugation and (de)stabilization β ↔
    βσ_n^{±1} ∈ B_{n+1}. Components of β̂ = cycles of the permutation of β.
  - TEMPERLEY-LIEB TL_n(d): generators e₁, ..., e_{n−1}, eᵢ² = d eᵢ,
    eᵢeᵢ±₁eᵢ = eᵢ, eᵢeⱼ = eⱼeᵢ (|i − j| ≥ 2); basis = planar matchings,
    dim = Catalan number (1, 2, 5, 14, 42, ...). σᵢ ↦ A + A⁻¹eᵢ is a
    representation of B_n and ⟨β̂⟩ = trace (closure: each loop counts d, then
    divide by d). JONES-WENZL idempotents fₙ (f₂ = 1 − e₁/d), with closures
    Δₙ: Δ₀ = 1, Δ₁ = d, Δₙ₊₁ = dΔₙ − Δₙ₋₁ (Chebyshev); for d = 2cos(π/r),
    Δₙ = sin((n+1)π/r)/sin(π/r), and Δ_{r−1} = 0 (truncation).
  - QUANTUM INTEGERS [n] = (qⁿ − q⁻ⁿ)/(q − q⁻¹) = q^{n−1} + q^{n−3} + ... +
    q^{1−n}; [n]! and q-binomials; [m][n] = ∑_{j=0}^{min−1} [m + n − 1 − 2j];
    at q = e^{πi/r}: [n] = sin(nπ/r)/sin(π/r), [r] = 0.
  - U_q(sl₂): generators E, F, K^{±1}, KEK⁻¹ = q²E, KFK⁻¹ = q⁻²F, EF − FE
    = (K − K⁻¹)/(q − q⁻¹). HOPF ALGEBRA: Δ(K) = K ⊗ K, Δ(E) = E ⊗ K + 1 ⊗ E,
    Δ(F) = F ⊗ 1 + K⁻¹ ⊗ F, ε(E) = ε(F) = 0, ε(K) = 1, S(K) = K⁻¹, S(E) =
    −EK⁻¹, S(F) = −KF (not cocommutative). For generic q the irreducible
    type-1 modules are Vₙ (dim n + 1, weights q^{n}, q^{n−2}, ..., q^{−n});
    Vₘ ⊗ Vₙ ≅ V_{m+n} ⊕ V_{m+n−2} ⊕ ... ⊕ V_{|m−n|}; quantum dimension
    Tr_{Vₙ}(K) = [n + 1]; Casimir C = EF + (q⁻¹K + qK⁻¹)/(q − q⁻¹)² acts on
    Vₙ by (q^{n+1} + q^{−n−1})/(q − q⁻¹)².
  - R-MATRICES: a (quasi)triangular Hopf algebra has an invertible R with
    Δᵒᵖ = RΔR⁻¹, and Ř = P∘R on V ⊗ V satisfies the YANG-BAXTER EQUATION
    Ř₁Ř₂Ř₁ = Ř₂Ř₁Ř₂, giving representations of B_n on V^{⊗n}. For U_q(sl₂)
    on V₁ ⊗ V₁ (basis 00, 01, 10, 11), up to the scalar q^{−1/2}:
       Ř = [[q, 0, 0, 0], [0, 0, 1, 0], [0, 1, q − q⁻¹, 0], [0, 0, 0, q]],
    HECKE relation (Ř − q)(Ř + q⁻¹) = 0, eigenvalues q (on the triplet, ×3)
    and −q⁻¹ (on the singlet). With μ = diag(q, q⁻¹) (the action of K),
    q^{−2w(β)} Tr(μ^{⊗n}ρ(β)) = (q + q⁻¹)·V_{β̂}(t) at t^{1/2} = −q⁻¹.
  - RESHETIKHIN-TURAEV: a RIBBON category (braiding c, twist θ, duals)
    gives a functor from framed coloured tangles to the category; for
    U_q(g)-modules this yields the COLOURED JONES polynomials (unknot
    coloured Vₙ ↦ [n + 1]) and, for U_q(sl_N), HOMFLY specialisations. At q
    = e^{πi/(k+2)} one truncates to the MODULAR category SU(2)_k: labels
    j = 0, ..., k, dⱼ = [j + 1], θⱼ = exp(2πi j(j+2)/(4(k+2))), S_{ij} =
    √(2/(k+2)) sin(π(i+1)(j+1)/(k+2)), D² = ∑dⱼ² = (k+2)/(2 sin²(π/(k+2))),
    fusion Vᵢ ⊗ Vⱼ = ⊕ V_m, |i − j| ≤ m ≤ min(i + j, 2k − i − j), i + j + m
    even. Hopf link coloured (i, j) ↦ S_{ij}/S₀₀.
  - RT 3-MANIFOLD INVARIANTS: every closed oriented 3-manifold is surgery
    M_L on a framed link L ⊂ S³ (Lickorish-Wallace), unique up to KIRBY
    moves. Z(M_L) = D^{−n−1} ζ^{−σ(L)} ∑_c ∏ d_{c(i)} J_L(c), n = #L, σ =
    signature of the linking matrix, ζ = p₊/D = e^{2πi c/8}, p± = ∑ θⱼ^{±1}
    dⱼ². Z(S³) = S₀₀ = 1/D, Z(S² × S¹) = 1, Z(Σ × S¹) = dim V(Σ), Z(M # N) =
    Z(M)Z(N)/Z(S³). WITTEN: this is SU(2) Chern-Simons theory at level k.
  - ATIYAH AXIOMS: an n-dim TQFT is a symmetric monoidal functor Z : Cobₙ →
    Vect_C: closed (n−1)-manifolds Σ ↦ Z(Σ), cobordisms ↦ linear maps,
    Z(∅) = C, Z(Σ ⊔ Σ′) = Z(Σ) ⊗ Z(Σ′), Z(Σ̄) = Z(Σ)*, gluing ↦ composition,
    Z(Σ × I) = id. Consequences: Z(Σ) finite dimensional, Z(M) ∈ C for
    closed M, Z(Σ × S¹) = dim Z(Σ), Z(mapping torus of f) = Tr Z(f).
  - 2D TQFT = COMMUTATIVE FROBENIUS ALGEBRA (A = Z(S¹), m = pants, unit =
    cap, counit ε = cup, nondegenerate pairing ε(ab)). HANDLE ELEMENT ω =
    m∘Δ(1) = ∑ bᵢbⁱ (dual bases); Z(Σ_g) = ε(ω^g). Semisimple A = ⊕Ceᵢ,
    ε(eᵢ) = λᵢ: Δ(eᵢ) = eᵢ ⊗ eᵢ/λᵢ, ω = ∑eᵢ/λᵢ, Z(Σ_g) = ∑ λᵢ^{1−g}.
    Examples: C[x]/(x²), ε(x) = 1 (Khovanov's algebra, ω = 2x); cohomology
    rings of closed even-dimensional manifolds (ω = χ·[pt]).
  - DIJKGRAAF-WITTEN (finite gauge group G): Z(M) = ∑_{P} 1/|Aut P| =
    |Hom(π₁M, G)|/|G| for closed connected M; Z(Σ) = functions on
    iso-classes of G-bundles (dim = |Hom(π₁Σ, G)/G|). 2D: Z(Σ_g) = ∑_ρ
    (|G|/dim ρ)^{2g−2} (FROBENIUS-MEDNYKH), from the centre of C[G]. 3D:
    Z(S³) = 1/|G|, Z(L(p, q)) = #{g : gᵖ = 1}/|G|, Z(T²) has dimension =
    #irreps of the Drinfeld double D(G). Twisted versions use ω ∈
    H^n(BG; U(1)).
  - TURAEV-VIRO: state sum over a triangulation with edges coloured by
    labels of a spherical fusion category C, weights dⱼ on edges, quantum
    6j-symbols on tetrahedra, D^{−2} per vertex; invariant under Pachner
    moves (Biedenharn-Elliott = pentagon, orthogonality). TV_C = RT_{Z(C)}
    (Drinfeld centre); for modular C, TV_C(M) = |RT_C(M)|² (Turaev-Walker,
    Roberts). For C = Vec_G, TV = DW.
  - VERLINDE FORMULA: N_{ab}^c = ∑_x S_{ax}S_{bx}S̄_{cx}/S_{0x}; the fusion
    matrices are diagonalised by S with eigenvalues S_{ax}/S_{0x}, and
    dim V(Σ_g) = ∑_x S_{0x}^{2−2g}. SU(2)_k: g = 1 gives k + 1, g = 2 gives
    C(k+3, 3); SU(2)_1: 2^g; SU(2)_2: 2^{g−1}(2^g + 1); Fibonacci: 1, 2, 5,
    15, 50, ... Equivalently: number of admissible colourings of a
    trivalent spine (pants decomposition).
  - KHOVANOV HOMOLOGY: cube of resolutions of an n-crossing diagram (n₊
    positive, n₋ negative), V = Z⟨1, X⟩, deg 1 = +1, deg X = −1, merge m:
    1·1 = 1, 1·X = X, X·X = 0; split Δ(1) = 1⊗X + X⊗1, Δ(X) = X⊗X. Chain
    group in degree r: ⊕_{|s|=r} V^{⊗k(s)}, homological shift −n₋, quantum
    shift r + n₊ − 2n₋. Kh^{i,j}(L) is a link invariant with ∑(−1)^i q^j
    rk Kh^{i,j} = (q + q⁻¹)V_L(t) at t^{1/2} = −q. Kh(O) = q + q⁻¹, Kh(right
    trefoil) = q + q³ + t²q⁵ + t³q⁹ (over Q; Z/2 torsion in (3, 7)).
  - KHOVANOV PROPERTIES: functorial under cobordisms (up to sign);
    detects the unknot (Kronheimer-Mrowka); strictly stronger than V (5₁ vs
    10₁₃₂); for alternating (thin) knots Kh(Q) is determined by V and the
    signature σ and rk Kh = det + 1. LEE's deformation gives a spectral
    sequence to Q ⊕ Q in degrees q^{s±1}: RASMUSSEN s(K) with |s| ≤ 2g₄(K),
    s(T(p, q)) = (p − 1)(q − 1) ⇒ g₄(T(p, q)) = (p − 1)(q − 1)/2 (MILNOR).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Prove that the Kauffman bracket is invariant under R2 and R3 and
    that R1 multiplies it by −A^{±3}; deduce that X(D) = (−A³)^{−w(D)}⟨D⟩ is
    an invariant of oriented links. (b) Compute ⟨D⟩, X and V for the
    standard diagram of the right-handed trefoil (w = +3) from the state
    sum. (c) Deduce that the trefoil is not isotopic to its mirror image.
    Answer: (b) ⟨D⟩ = A⁻⁷ − A⁻³ − A⁵, X = A⁻⁴ + A⁻¹² − A⁻¹⁶, V = t + t³ −
    t⁴. (c) V(left trefoil) = t⁻¹ + t⁻³ − t⁻⁴ ≠ V(right trefoil).
    Teacher explains: (a) R2: expand both crossings of the two-strand
    picture. The four states give A·A⁻¹⟨ || ⟩ (vertical smoothing), A²⟨ ⊃⊂ ⟩,
    A⁻²⟨ ⊃⊂ ⟩ and A⁻¹·A⟨ ⊃○⊂ ⟩ = d⟨ ⊃⊂ ⟩, where ⊃⊂ is the horizontal
    cup-cap. Total: ⟨ || ⟩ + (A² + A⁻² + d)⟨ ⊃⊂ ⟩ = ⟨ || ⟩ because d = −A² −
    A⁻². This is exactly why d is chosen that way. R3: expand the crossing
    NOT involved in the move; the A-terms of both sides are R2-equivalent
    pictures and the B-terms are identical after planar isotopy, so R3
    follows from R2. R1: a kink gives ⟨kink⟩ = A⟨|○⟩ + A⁻¹⟨|⟩ = (Ad + A⁻¹)
    ⟨|⟩ = −A³⟨|⟩ for one type, and −A⁻³⟨|⟩ for the other. As R1 changes
    w by ±1 correspondingly, (−A³)^{−w}⟨D⟩ is unchanged; w is invariant
    under R2, R3, so X is invariant under all moves. (b) Label states by
    a = number of A-smoothings. For this diagram the all-A state is the
    oriented (Seifert) resolution with 2 loops; changing one smoothing
    merges or splits: a = 3: 2 loops (1 state); a = 2: 1 loop (3 states);
    a = 1: 2 loops (3 states); a = 0: 3 loops (1 state). Hence
       ⟨D⟩ = A³d + 3A + 3A⁻¹d + A⁻³d².
    Now A³d = −A⁵ − A, 3A⁻¹d = −3A − 3A⁻³, A⁻³d² = A⁻³(A⁴ + 2 + A⁻⁴) = A +
    2A⁻³ + A⁻⁷. Sum: −A⁵ + (−1 + 3 − 3 + 1)A + (−3 + 2)A⁻³ + A⁻⁷ = A⁻⁷ − A⁻³
    − A⁵. Then X = (−A³)^{−3}⟨D⟩ = −A⁻⁹⟨D⟩ = −A⁻¹⁶ + A⁻¹² + A⁻⁴. With A⁻⁴ = t:
    V = t + t³ − t⁴. (c) The mirror diagram has all crossings switched, so
    A ↔ A⁻¹ and w ↦ −w: V(K̄)(t) = V(K)(t⁻¹) = t⁻¹ + t⁻³ − t⁻⁴. These differ,
    so no sequence of Reidemeister moves joins the two trefoils.
    Common mistakes: forgetting the d^{|s|−1} normalisation (⟨O⟩ = 1, not
    d); using X with (−A³)^{+w}; concluding amphichirality from a symmetric
    V (V(K)(t) = V(K)(t⁻¹) is necessary, not sufficient, for K ≅ K̄).
    Now you try: compute ⟨Hopf link⟩ and V(H₊) for the positive Hopf link.
    (⟨H⟩ = −A⁴ − A⁻⁴; X = A⁻⁶(−A⁴ − A⁻⁴), V(H₊) = −t^{1/2} − t^{5/2})

D2. (a) Derive the Jones skein relation t⁻¹V(L₊) − tV(L₋) = (t^{1/2} −
    t^{−1/2})V(L₀) from the bracket. (b) Let Tₙ = T(2, n) be the closure of
    σ₁ⁿ with parallel strands (all crossings positive). Show Vₙ = t²Vₙ₋₂ +
    t(t^{1/2} − t^{−1/2})Vₙ₋₁ and compute V₂, V₃, V₄, V₅. (c) Check V(4₁)
    using a positive crossing whose change gives the unknot and whose
    smoothing gives the negative Hopf link H₋.
    Answer: (b) with s = t^{1/2}: V₂ = −s − s⁵, V₃ = s² + s⁶ − s⁸, V₄ = −s³ −
    s⁷ + s⁹ − s¹¹, V₅ = s⁴ + s⁸ − s¹⁰ + s¹² − s¹⁴ = t² + t⁴ − t⁵ + t⁶ − t⁷.
    (c) consistent: t⁻¹V(4₁) − t = t⁻³ − t⁻² + t⁻¹ − 1 = (s − s⁻¹)V(H₋).
    Teacher explains: (a) At a positive crossing the A-smoothing is the
    oriented smoothing L₀ and the B-smoothing is the unoriented L_∞; at a
    negative crossing it is the other way round:
       ⟨L₊⟩ = A⟨L₀⟩ + A⁻¹⟨L_∞⟩,   ⟨L₋⟩ = A⁻¹⟨L₀⟩ + A⟨L_∞⟩.
    Eliminate L_∞: A⟨L₊⟩ − A⁻¹⟨L₋⟩ = (A² − A⁻²)⟨L₀⟩. Write ⟨L⟩ = (−A³)^{w}X(L)
    with w(L±) = w₀ ± 1 and divide by (−A³)^{w₀}: −A⁴X₊ + A⁻⁴X₋ = (A² −
    A⁻²)X₀. Put A⁴ = t⁻¹, A² − A⁻² = t^{−1/2} − t^{1/2} and change sign.
    (b) At a crossing of Tₙ: L₊ = Tₙ, L₋ = Tₙ₋₂ (the crossing cancels its
    neighbour by R2), L₀ = Tₙ₋₁. So Vₙ = t²Vₙ₋₂ + t(s − s⁻¹)Vₙ₋₁, with V₀ =
    V(2-unlink) = −s − s⁻¹ and V₁ = 1. Note t(s − s⁻¹) = s³ − s.
       V₂ = s⁴(−s − s⁻¹) + (s³ − s) = −s⁵ − s,
       V₃ = s⁴ + (s³ − s)(−s⁵ − s) = s⁴ − s⁸ − s⁴ + s⁶ + s² = s² + s⁶ − s⁸,
       V₄ = s⁴(−s⁵ − s) + (s³ − s)(s² + s⁶ − s⁸) = −s³ − s⁷ + s⁹ − s¹¹,
       V₅ = s⁴V₃ + (s³ − s)V₄ = s⁴ + s⁸ − s¹⁰ + s¹² − s¹⁴.
    V₃ is the trefoil t + t³ − t⁴ and V₅ agrees with the torus-knot formula
    t²(1 − t³ − t⁶ + t⁷)/(1 − t²) = t² + t⁴ − t⁵ + t⁶ − t⁷. (c) With V(4₁) =
    t⁻² − t⁻¹ + 1 − t + t²: t⁻¹V(4₁) − t·1 = t⁻³ − t⁻² + t⁻¹ − 1, and (s −
    s⁻¹)(−s⁻¹ − s⁻⁵) = −1 + s⁻² − s⁻⁴ + s⁻⁶ = t⁻³ − t⁻² + t⁻¹ − 1. ✓
    Common mistakes: using L₀ = T_{n−1} with the wrong orientation (for
    antiparallel strands T(2, n) has negative crossings); forgetting that
    links with an even number of components have half-integer powers.
    Now you try: compute V₆ = V(T(2, 6)).
    (−t^{5/2} − t^{9/2} + t^{11/2} − t^{13/2} + t^{15/2} − t^{17/2})

D3. (a) Prove V_L(1) = (−2)^{c−1} for a c-component link, and check
    |V(−1)| = det, V(e^{2πi/3}) = 1 and V(i) = ±1 on 3₁, 4₁, 5₂, where
    V(5₂) = t⁻¹ − t⁻² + 2t⁻³ − t⁻⁴ + t⁻⁵ − t⁻⁶. (b) Prove span⟨D⟩ ≤ 4n for a
    connected n-crossing diagram and explain why equality holds for
    reduced alternating diagrams. (c) Deduce crossing numbers of 3₁, 4₁,
    5₂ and show 8₁₉ = T(3, 4) is not alternating.
    Answer: (a) dets 3, 5, 7; V(ω) = 1 for all; V(i) = −1, −1, +1.
    (b) proof below. (c) c = 3, 4, 5; span V(8₁₉) = 5 < 8.
    Teacher explains: (a) At t = 1 (s = 1) the skein reads V(L₊) − V(L₋)
    = 0, so V(1) is unchanged by crossing changes. Any diagram can be
    unknotted and unlinked by crossing changes into the c-component
    unlink, whose value is (−s − s⁻¹)^{c−1} = (−2)^{c−1}. Checks: V(3₁)(−1)
    = −1 − 1 − 1 = −3; V(4₁)(−1) = 1 + 1 + 1 + 1 + 1 = 5; V(5₂)(−1) = −1 −
    1 − 2 − 1 − 1 − 1 = −7. At ω = e^{2πi/3} (ω² + ω + 1 = 0): V(3₁) = ω +
    1 − ω = 1; V(4₁) = ω⁻² − ω⁻¹ + 1 − ω + ω² = ω − ω² + 1 − ω + ω² = 1.
    At t = i: V(3₁) = i − i − 1 = −1 (Arf = 1,
    det 3 ≡ 3 mod 8); V(4₁) = −1 + i + 1 − i − 1 = −1 (det 5); V(5₂) =
    −i + 1 + 2i − 1 − i + 1 = 1 (Arf = 0, det 7 ≡ −1 mod 8). (b) A state
    s contributes degrees ≤ a − b + 2|s| − 2. Passing from s_A (all A) to
    any s by switching one smoothing at a time, a − b drops by 2 and the
    loop count changes by ±1, so the bound never increases: max deg ≤ n +
    2|s_A| − 2. For an ADEQUATE diagram (switching one smoothing of s_A
    always merges two loops), e.g. reduced alternating, only s_A reaches
    it, with coefficient (−1)^{|s_A|−1}. Similarly min deg ≥ −n − 2|s_B| +
    2. So span ≤ 2n + 2(|s_A| + |s_B|) − 4. For a
    connected diagram |s_A| + |s_B| ≤ n + 2 (Euler characteristic of the
    associated ribbon surface / checkerboard graph and its dual), with
    equality iff the diagram is alternating. Hence span⟨D⟩ ≤ 4n, and for a
    reduced alternating diagram the extreme coefficients are ±1, so span =
    4n and span V = n. (c) spans: 3₁: 4 − 1 = 3; 4₁: 2 − (−2) = 4; 5₂:
    −1 − (−6) = 5. Since any diagram has span V ≤ #crossings, these are the
    crossing numbers. For 8₁₉, span V = 8 − 3 = 5 < 8 = c(8₁₉); an
    alternating knot would have span V = c, so 8₁₉ is not alternating.
    Common mistakes: using span V ≤ n to get a LOWER bound for c (it gives
    c ≥ span V); forgetting that V(i) = 0 for links with odd linking, e.g.
    Hopf: −s⁵ − s at s = e^{iπ/4} is 0.
    Now you try: det and crossing number of 5₁ from V = t² + t⁴ − t⁵ + t⁶ −
    t⁷. (det = |1 + 1 + 1 + 1 + 1| = 5; span 5, alternating, c = 5)

D4. (a) Show that ρ(σᵢ) = A + A⁻¹eᵢ, ρ(σᵢ⁻¹) = A⁻¹ + Aeᵢ defines a
    representation of B_n in TL_n(d), d = −A² − A⁻². (b) Compute ⟨σ̂₁²⟩ and
    ⟨σ̂₁³⟩ in TL₂ using closure (identity ↦ d, e₁ ↦ 1). (c) Show that f₂ =
    1 − e₁/d is an idempotent killed by e₁, compute Δ₂, Δ₃, and find where
    they vanish for d = 2cos(π/r). Give dim TL₃ and a basis.
    Answer: (b) −A⁴ − A⁻⁴ and A⁻⁷ − A⁻³ − A⁵. (c) Δ₂ = d² − 1, Δ₃ = d³ −
    2d; Δ₂ = 0 at d = 1 (r = 3), Δ₃ = 0 at d = √2 (r = 4). dim TL₃ = 5:
    1, e₁, e₂, e₁e₂, e₂e₁.
    Teacher explains: (a) Inverse: (A + A⁻¹e)(A⁻¹ + Ae) = 1 + (A² + A⁻²)e +
    e² = 1 + (A² + A⁻² + d)e = 1. Far commutativity is clear. Braid
    relation: expand, using e₁² = de₁, e₁e₂e₁ = e₁:
       ρ(σ₁σ₂σ₁) = A³ + A(2e₁ + e₂) + A⁻¹(e₁e₂ + e₂e₁ + de₁) + A⁻³e₁,
       ρ(σ₂σ₁σ₂) = A³ + A(2e₂ + e₁) + A⁻¹(e₂e₁ + e₁e₂ + de₂) + A⁻³e₂.
    Difference = (A + A⁻¹d + A⁻³)(e₁ − e₂) = (A − A − A⁻³ + A⁻³)(e₁ − e₂)
    = 0. (The same d as for R2 makes the braid relation hold.) (b) ρ(σ₁²) =
    A² + 2e₁ + A⁻²de₁ = A² + (1 − A⁻⁴)e₁. Closure: A²d + (1 − A⁻⁴) = −A⁴ − 1
    + 1 − A⁻⁴ = −A⁴ − A⁻⁴. ρ(σ₁³) = A³ + (3A + 3A⁻¹d + A⁻³d²)e₁; closing,
    ⟨σ̂₁³⟩ = A³d + 3A + 3A⁻¹d + A⁻³d², the state sum of D1: A⁻⁷ − A⁻³ − A⁵,
    the RIGHT trefoil (positive braid). (c) f₂² = 1 − 2e₁/d + e₁²/d² = 1 −
    2e₁/d + e₁/d = f₂; e₁f₂ = e₁ − e₁²/d = 0. Closing f₂ in the plane (each
    loop counts d): closure(1) = d², closure(e₁) = d, so Δ₂ = d² − d/d =
    d² − 1. Recursion Δ₃ = dΔ₂ − Δ₁ = d³ − 2d. With d = 2cos θ, Δₙ =
    sin((n+1)θ)/sin θ: θ = π/3 gives Δ₂ = sin π/sin(π/3) = 0; θ = π/4
    gives Δ₃ = 0. At such roots of unity f_{r−1} has zero trace: this is
    the TRUNCATION that makes SU(2)_k finite (r = k + 2). TL₃ basis:
    planar matchings of 3 + 3 points: 5 = Catalan C₃.
    Common mistakes: normalising closures inconsistently (⟨O⟩ = 1 means
    divide the loop count d^{#loops} by d); forgetting writhe when passing
    from ⟨β̂⟩ to V.
    Now you try: ⟨σ̂₁⁻³⟩ and dim TL₄. (A⁷ − A³ − A⁻⁵, the left trefoil; 14)

D5. (a) On Vₙ with basis v₀, ..., vₙ put Kvᵢ = q^{n−2i}vᵢ, Fvᵢ = vᵢ₊₁ (vₙ₊₁ =
    0), Evᵢ = [i][n − i + 1]vᵢ₋₁. Verify EF − FE = (K − K⁻¹)/(q − q⁻¹) and
    compute the Casimir eigenvalue. (b) Decompose V₁ ⊗ V₁ using Δ(E) = E ⊗
    K + 1 ⊗ E; find the singlet. (c) Check dimension and quantum-dimension
    counts for V₁ ⊗ V₁ and V₁ ⊗ V₂.
    Answer: (a) Casimir = (q^{n+1} + q^{−n−1})/(q − q⁻¹)². (b) V₁ ⊗ V₁ ≅ V₂
    ⊕ V₀, singlet qv₀ ⊗ v₁ − v₁ ⊗ v₀. (c) [2]² = [3] + [1]; [2][3] = [4] +
    [2].
    Teacher explains: (a) KE and EK: E lowers i, raising the weight by q²,
    fine. On vᵢ: EFvᵢ = [i + 1][n − i]vᵢ and FEvᵢ = [i][n − i + 1]vᵢ. Claim
    [i + 1][n − i] − [i][n − i + 1] = [n − 2i]. Multiply by (q − q⁻¹)²:
       (q^{i+1} − q^{−i−1})(q^{n−i} − q^{i−n}) = q^{n+1} − q^{2i+1−n} −
       q^{n−2i−1} + q^{−n−1},
       (qⁱ − q⁻ⁱ)(q^{n−i+1} − q^{i−n−1}) = q^{n+1} − q^{2i−n−1} −
       q^{n−2i+1} + q^{−n−1}.
    Difference: q^{n−2i}(q − q⁻¹) − q^{2i−n}(q − q⁻¹) = (q − q⁻¹)²[n − 2i],
    and (K − K⁻¹)/(q − q⁻¹) acts on vᵢ by [n − 2i]. ✓ Casimir: on the
    highest vector v₀ (Ev₀ = 0) EFv₀ = [n]v₀, so C = [n] + (q^{n−1} +
    q^{1−n})/(q − q⁻¹)². Since [n](q − q⁻¹)² = q^{n+1} − q^{n−1} − q^{1−n} +
    q^{−n−1}, the sum is (q^{n+1} + q^{−n−1})/(q − q⁻¹)²; C is central, so
    this holds on all of Vₙ.
    (b) On V₁: Ev₁ = v₀, Kv₀ = qv₀, Kv₁ = q⁻¹v₁. Weights on V₁ ⊗ V₁ (K
    acts by K ⊗ K): q², 1, 1, q⁻². v₀ ⊗ v₀ is highest of weight q² and
    generates a copy of V₂. For w = a v₀⊗v₁ + b v₁⊗v₀:
       Δ(E)(v₀⊗v₁) = Ev₀⊗Kv₁ + v₀⊗Ev₁ = v₀⊗v₀,
       Δ(E)(v₁⊗v₀) = Ev₁⊗Kv₀ + v₁⊗Ev₀ = q v₀⊗v₀.
    So Δ(E)w = 0 iff a + qb = 0: w = qv₀⊗v₁ − v₁⊗v₀ spans V₀ (weight 1).
    Note the asymmetry: at q = 1 it is the ordinary antisymmetric singlet.
    (c) dims 4 = 3 + 1, 6 = 4 + 2; quantum dims (trace of K): (q + q⁻¹)² =
    q² + 2 + q⁻² = [3] + [1], and (q + q⁻¹)(q² + 1 + q⁻²) = q³ + 2q + 2q⁻¹
    + q⁻³ = [4] + [2].
    Common mistakes: Δ(E) = E ⊗ 1 + 1 ⊗ E (that is the classical coproduct
    and is not an algebra map here); using the ordinary dimension instead
    of Tr(K) as the quantum dimension.
    Now you try: decompose V₂ ⊗ V₂ and check with quantum integers.
    (V₄ ⊕ V₂ ⊕ V₀; [3]² = [5] + [3] + [1] = q⁴ + 2q² + 3 + 2q⁻² + q⁻⁴)

D6. (a) For Ř = [[q,0,0,0],[0,0,1,0],[0,1,q − q⁻¹,0],[0,0,0,q]] prove the
    Hecke relation, find the eigenvalues and Ř⁻¹. (Ř satisfies the YBE; it
    is PR for U_q(sl₂) up to a scalar.) (b) With μ = diag(q, q⁻¹) show
    Tr₂((1 ⊗ μ)Ř^{±1}) = q^{±2}·id, and deduce that I(β̂) = q^{−2w(β)}
    Tr(μ^{⊗n}ρ(β)) is invariant under both Markov moves. (c) Compute I for
    β = σ₁ⁿ ∈ B₂ and compare with V(T(2, n)).
    Answer: (a) eigenvalues q, q, q, −q⁻¹; Ř⁻¹ = Ř − (q − q⁻¹)·1. (c) I =
    [3]q⁻ⁿ + (−1)ⁿq⁻³ⁿ = (q + q⁻¹)V(T(2, n)) at t = q⁻² (t^{1/2} = −q⁻¹).
    Teacher explains: (a) Ř is diagonal (entry q) on 00 and 11 and on
    span(01, 10) is B = [[0, 1], [1, q − q⁻¹]], with trace q − q⁻¹ and det
    −1, so eigenvalues q and −q⁻¹ (eigenvectors (1, q) and (1, −q⁻¹)).
    Hence (Ř − q)(Ř + q⁻¹) = 0, i.e. Ř² = (q − q⁻¹)Ř + 1, so Ř⁻¹ = Ř − (q −
    q⁻¹). The q-eigenspace (dim 3) is V₂, the −q⁻¹ one is V₀. (b) Ř maps
    00 ↦ q·00, 01 ↦ 10, 10 ↦ 01 + (q − q⁻¹)10, 11 ↦ q·11. The partial trace
    over the second factor is Pᵢⱼ = ∑ₖ μₖ Ř_{(i,k),(j,k)}: P₀₀ = q·q + q⁻¹·0
    = q², P₁₁ = q(q − q⁻¹) + q⁻¹·q = q², off-diagonal entries vanish. For
    Ř⁻¹: q² − (q − q⁻¹)Tr μ = q² − (q − q⁻¹)(q + q⁻¹) = q⁻². Markov I
    (conjugation): trace is cyclic and μ^{⊗n} commutes with ρ(β) (Ř commutes
    with μ ⊗ μ = K ⊗ K). Markov II: closing the last strand of βσₙ^{±1}
    replaces Tr(μ^{⊗(n+1)}ρ(β)Řₙ^{±1}) by q^{±2}Tr(μ^{⊗n}ρ(β)), exactly
    cancelled by q^{−2w}. (c) μ ⊗ μ has trace q² + q⁻² + 1 = [3] on the
    triplet (weights q², q⁻², 1) and 1 on the singlet, so Tr(μ⊗μ Řⁿ) =
    [3]qⁿ + (−q⁻¹)ⁿ, and I = q^{−2n}(...) = [3]q⁻ⁿ + (−1)ⁿq⁻³ⁿ. n = 1:
    [3]q⁻¹ − q⁻³ = q + q⁻¹ (unknot ↦ [2]). n = 3: q⁻¹ + q⁻³ + q⁻⁵ − q⁻⁹ =
    (q + q⁻¹)(q⁻² + q⁻⁶ − q⁻⁸), i.e. t + t³ − t⁴ at t = q⁻². n = 2: 1 + q⁻²
    + q⁻⁴ + q⁻⁶ = (q + q⁻¹)(q⁻¹ + q⁻⁵), and −s − s⁵ at s = −q⁻¹ gives q⁻¹ +
    q⁻⁵. ✓
    Common mistakes: taking the ordinary trace (μ = 1), which is not a
    Markov trace; forgetting that ρ(σᵢ⁻¹) = Ř⁻¹, not Řᵀ.
    Now you try: I(σ̂₁⁵) and the normalised quotient.
    (q⁻³ + q⁻⁵ + q⁻⁷ − q⁻¹⁵; q⁻⁴ + q⁻⁸ − q⁻¹⁰ + q⁻¹² − q⁻¹⁴ = V(T(2, 5)))

D7. (a) State the Atiyah axioms for an n-dimensional TQFT Z. Prove that
    every Z(Σ) is finite dimensional and that Z(Σ × S¹) = dim Z(Σ). (b)
    Classify oriented 1-dimensional TQFTs; for Z(pt₊) = C³ give Z(S¹) and
    Z(S¹ ⊔ S¹ ⊔ S¹). (c) Assume Z(S^{n−1}) = C and Z(Sⁿ) ≠ 0. Prove Z(M # N)
    = Z(M)Z(N)/Z(Sⁿ), and check it for 3D Dijkgraaf-Witten theory with G =
    Z/2 and M = N = RP³.
    Answer: (a), (c) proofs below. (b) Z ↔ the finite-dimensional vector
    space Z(pt₊); Z(S¹) = 3, Z(3 circles) = 27. (c) Z(RP³ # RP³) = 2 =
    1·1/(1/2).
    Teacher explains: (a) Axioms: Z assigns a vector space Z(Σ) to each
    closed oriented (n−1)-manifold and a linear map Z(W) : Z(Σ₀) → Z(Σ₁) to
    each cobordism W (up to diffeomorphism rel boundary), such that gluing
    goes to composition, Z(Σ × I) = id, Z(∅) = C, Z(Σ ⊔ Σ′) = Z(Σ) ⊗ Z(Σ′)
    (symmetric monoidal) and Z(Σ̄) = Z(Σ)*. A closed M is a cobordism ∅ →
    ∅, so Z(M) ∈ End(C) = C. Bending the cylinder Σ × I gives ev : Z(Σ) ⊗
    Z(Σ̄) → C and coev : C → Z(Σ̄) ⊗ Z(Σ), and the S-shaped (zig-zag)
    cylinder is diffeomorphic to the straight one, so (ev ⊗ id)(id ⊗ coev)
    = id. Write coev(1) = ∑_{i=1}^N xᵢ ⊗ yᵢ (a FINITE sum); then every v =
    ∑ ev(v ⊗ xᵢ)yᵢ lies in span(y₁, ..., y_N): dim Z(Σ) ≤ N < ∞. Finally
    Σ × S¹ = (Σ × I bent up) glued to (Σ × I bent down): Z(Σ × S¹) =
    ev∘coev(1) = trace of id_{Z(Σ)} = dim Z(Σ). (b) Objects are finite sets
    of signed points, so Z is determined by V = Z(pt₊) (then Z(pt₋) = V*);
    the two bent intervals are ev and coev, and S¹ = ev∘coev ↦ dim V.
    Conversely every finite-dimensional V works. For V = C³: Z(S¹) = 3 and
    by monoidality Z(S¹ ⊔ S¹ ⊔ S¹) = 3³ = 27. (c) Cut M # N along the
    separating sphere: M # N = M° ∪_{S^{n−1}} N°, where M° = M minus a ball.
    Also M = M° ∪ Bⁿ, N = N° ∪ Bⁿ, Sⁿ = Bⁿ ∪ Bⁿ. In the one-dimensional
    Z(S^{n−1}) = C·e write Z(M°) = m·e, Z(N°) = n·e* and Z(B) = β·e or β′·e*
    (depending on the side). Then Z(M) = mβ′, Z(N) = nβ, Z(Sⁿ) = ββ′ and
    Z(M # N) = mn. Hence Z(M # N)Z(Sⁿ) = mnββ′ = Z(M)Z(N). DW check: Z(M)
    = |Hom(π₁M, G)|/|G|. Z(S³) = 1/2; Z(RP³) = |Hom(Z/2, Z/2)|/2 = 1;
    π₁(RP³ # RP³) = Z/2 * Z/2 (free product) has 2·2 = 4 homomorphisms to
    Z/2, so Z = 4/2 = 2 = 1·1/(1/2). ✓
    Common mistakes: expecting Z(M # N) = Z(M)Z(N) (true only if Z(Sⁿ) =
    1); forgetting that Z(Σ̄) is the DUAL space (orientation matters: Z(M̄)
    = conjugate of Z(M) in unitary theories); allowing infinite dimensions.
    Now you try: in DW theory with |G| = n compute Z(S² × S¹ # S² × S¹)
    both directly and by the formula. (π₁ = F₂: n²/n = n; 1·1/(1/n) = n)

D8. (a) Show that a 2D TQFT makes A = Z(S¹) a commutative Frobenius
    algebra and that Z(Σ_g) = ε(ω^g), ω = m∘Δ(1). (b) For semisimple A =
    ⊕ᵢ Ceᵢ with ε(eᵢ) = λᵢ ≠ 0 compute Δ, ω and Z(Σ_g). (c) Compute Z(Σ_g),
    g = 0, 1, 2, 3, for A = C[x]/(x²) with ε(x) = 1, ε(1) = 0 (Khovanov's
    algebra), for A = C[x]/(x² − 1) with the same ε (Lee's algebra), and for
    A = H*(CP²; C) = C[x]/(x³), ε(x²) = 1.
    Answer: (b) Δ(eᵢ) = eᵢ ⊗ eᵢ/λᵢ, ω = ∑eᵢ/λᵢ, Z(Σ_g) = ∑λᵢ^{1−g}.
    (c) Khovanov: 0, 2, 0, 0; Lee: 0, 2, 0, 8; CP²: 0, 3, 0, 0.
    Teacher explains: (a) The pair of pants S¹ ⊔ S¹ → S¹ gives m, the disc
    ∅ → S¹ gives the unit, the disc S¹ → ∅ gives ε. Associativity and
    unit laws are diffeomorphisms of surfaces; composing pants with the
    swap of the two inputs gives a diffeomorphic surface, so m is
    COMMUTATIVE. The bent cylinder S¹ ⊔ S¹ → ∅ is ε∘m, and the zig-zag
    identity (D7) shows the pairing ⟨a, b⟩ = ε(ab) is NONDEGENERATE: A is a
    commutative Frobenius algebra. Conversely (Abrams, Dijkgraaf) the
    classification of surfaces gives generators and relations of 2Cob,
    so the two notions are equivalent. The upside-down pants give Δ, and
    Frobenius compatibility yields m∘Δ(a) = aω. A closed Σ_g = cap ∘
    (handle)^g ∘ cup, and each handle multiplies by ω: Z(Σ_g) = ε(ω^g).
    Check: g = 0 gives ε(1) = Z(S²), g = 1 gives ε(ω) = ∑ε(bᵢbⁱ) = dim A,
    the torus. (b) ⟨eᵢ, eⱼ⟩ = ε(eᵢeⱼ) = δᵢⱼλᵢ, so the dual basis is eⁱ =
    eᵢ/λᵢ and Δ(1) = ∑ eᵢ ⊗ eᵢ/λᵢ, Δ(eᵢ) = eᵢ ⊗ eᵢ/λᵢ; ω = ∑eᵢ/λᵢ, ω^g =
    ∑eᵢλᵢ^{−g}, Z(Σ_g) = ∑λᵢ^{1−g}. (c) Khovanov: pairing matrix on {1, x}
    is [[0, 1], [1, 0]], dual basis {x, 1}, so ω = 1·x + x·1 = 2x, ω² =
    4x² = 0: Z = ε(1), ε(2x), 0, 0 = 0, 2, 0, 0. (Δ(1) = 1⊗x + x⊗1 and
    Δ(x) = x⊗x, the Khovanov comultiplication.) Lee: same dual basis, ω =
    2x, ω² = 4, ω³ = 8x: Z = 0, 2, ε(4) = 0, ε(8x) = 8. Check with (b): e± =
    (1 ± x)/2, λ± = ±1/2, Z(Σ_g) = (1/2)^{1−g} + (−1/2)^{1−g}: g = 3 gives 4
    + 4 = 8 ✓. CP²: dual basis of (1, x, x²) is (x², x, 1), ω = 3x² = χ(CP²)
    ·[pt], ω² = 0: Z = 0, 3, 0, 0.
    Common mistakes: guessing Δ(x) = x ⊗ x for all bases (Δ is DETERMINED
    by ε); forgetting that ε must make the pairing nondegenerate (ε(1) =
    1, ε(x) = 0 on C[x]/(x²) is not Frobenius: x pairs to 0 with all).
    Now you try: A = C³ with λ = (1, 1, 1/2): Z(Σ_g) for g = 0, 1, 2, 3.
    (2 + 2^{g−1}: 5/2, 3, 4, 6)

D9. (a) Define untwisted Dijkgraaf-Witten theory for a finite group G and
    show Z(M) = |Hom(π₁M, G)|/|G| for closed connected M. (b) In 2D, prove
    Z(Σ_g) = ∑_ρ (|G|/dim ρ)^{2g−2} via the centre of C[G], and compute
    |Hom(π₁Σ₂, S₃)|. (c) In 3D compute Z(S³), Z(RP³), Z(L(3, 1)), Z(T³) for
    G = S₃, and show DW distinguishes D₄ from Q₈ although the 2D theory
    does not. Explain why the Turaev-Viro state sum for Vec_G gives DW.
    Answer: (b) 486. (c) 1/6, 2/3, 1/2, 8; Z(RP³) = 3/4 for D₄, 1/4 for Q₈.
    Teacher explains: (a) Z(M) = ∑ over iso-classes of principal G-bundles
    P → M of 1/|Aut P| (a groupoid cardinality: a finite "path integral"
    over flat G-connections). Bundles on connected M ↔ Hom(π₁M, G)/G
    (conjugation), and Aut P = centraliser of the image. By the orbit-
    stabiliser theorem ∑_{orbits} 1/|Stab| = |Hom|/|G|. State spaces are
    functions on bundles over Σ; gluing is a sum over bundles on the cut.
    (b) Z(S¹) = class functions = centre of C[G], with ε(∑a_g g) = a_e/|G|.
    Primitive central idempotents e_ρ = (dim ρ/|G|)∑χ_ρ(g⁻¹)g have λ_ρ =
    ε(e_ρ) = (dim ρ)²/|G|², so by D8(b) Z(Σ_g) = ∑_ρ (dim ρ/|G|)^{2−2g}.
    For S₃ (irreps of dims 1, 1, 2): Z(Σ_g) = 2·6^{2g−2} + 3^{2g−2}. g = 0:
    2/36 + 1/9 = 1/6 = 1/|G| ✓; g = 1: 3 = number of classes ✓ (commuting
    pairs/|G| = 18/6); g = 2: 72 + 9 = 81, so |Hom(π₁Σ₂, S₃)| = 81·6 = 486.
    (c) Z(S³) = 1/6. π₁(L(p, q)) = Z/p: Z = #{g : gᵖ = 1}/|G|. In S₃: 4
    elements with g² = 1, 3 with g³ = 1: Z(RP³) = 4/6 = 2/3, Z(L(3, 1)) =
    1/2. T³: Z = #commuting triples/|G| = number of G-orbits on commuting
    pairs = ∑_{classes [g]} k(C(g)) = k(S₃) + k(Z/2) + k(Z/3) = 3 + 2 + 3 =
    8 (= number of irreps of the Drinfeld double D(S₃) = dim Z(T²)).
    D₄ and Q₈ both have irreps of dims 1, 1, 1, 1, 2, so their 2D theories
    coincide (Z(Σ_g) = 4·8^{2g−2} + 4^{2g−2}). But D₄ has 6 solutions of g²
    = 1 and Q₈ only 2 (±1): Z(RP³) = 6/8 = 3/4 vs 2/8 = 1/4. TV for Vec_G:
    colour oriented edges of a triangulation by G with the product around
    every triangle = e (all dⱼ = 1, 6j-symbols = 1 on flat colourings),
    weight |G|^{−V}. Flat colourings = |G|^{V−1}|Hom(π₁M, G)| (a maximal
    tree has V − 1 free edges), so TV = |Hom|/|G| = DW. E.g. S³ = ∂Δ⁴: 5
    vertices, π₁ = 1, |G|⁴ colourings, TV = |G|⁴/|G|⁵ = 1/|G| ✓.
    Common mistakes: counting homomorphisms without dividing by |G|;
    confusing dim Z₂D(T²) = k(G) (= 3 for S₃) with the 3D state space
    dim Z₃D(T²) = 8; assuming Z(L(p, q)) depends on q (untwisted: it does
    not).
    Now you try: Z(T³) for D₄ and for Z/n; Z(Poincaré sphere) for S₃.
    (22; n²; 1/6, since π₁ is perfect and S₃ is solvable)

D10. (a) For SU(2)_k verify dⱼ = S₀ⱼ/S₀₀ = [j + 1], the normalised Hopf
    link S_{ij}/S₀₀ = [(i + 1)(j + 1)] at q = e^{πi/(k+2)}, and D² =
    (k + 2)/(2 sin²(π/(k + 2))). Evaluate for k = 3. (b) Using the surgery
    formula Z(M_L) = D^{−n−1}ζ^{−σ(L)}∑_c ∏d_{c(i)}J_L(c), check Z(S³) from
    the empty link and from the ±1-framed unknot, and Z(S² × S¹). (c) For
    k = 1 (d = 1, 1; θ = 1, i) compute Z(L(p, 1)) for p = 1, 2, 3, 4 (L(p, 1)
    = p-surgery on the unknot).
    Answer: (a) k = 3: d = 1, φ, φ, 1 (φ = golden ratio), D² = 5 + √5.
    (b) all give D⁻¹ = S₀₀ resp. 1. (c) ½e^{−iπ/4}(1 + iᵖ): 1/√2, 0, −i/√2,
    e^{−iπ/4} (up to complex conjugation for the opposite orientation).
    Teacher explains: (a) S₀ⱼ/S₀₀ = sin(π(j+1)/(k+2))/sin(π/(k+2)) = [j + 1]
    since [n] = sin(nπ/r)/sin(π/r) at q = e^{πi/r}, r = k + 2. Likewise
    S_{ij}/S₀₀ = sin(π(i+1)(j+1)/r)/sin(π/r) = [(i+1)(j+1)] (the Hopf link
    with colours Vᵢ, Vⱼ, as for generic q). D² = ∑_{m=1}^{r−1} sin²(mπ/r)/
    sin²(π/r) and ∑_{m=1}^{r−1} sin²(mπ/r) = r/2, so D² = r/(2 sin²(π/r)) and
    S₀₀ = √(2/r) sin(π/r) = 1/D. k = 3, r = 5: [2] = sin 72°/sin 36° =
    2cos 36° = φ, [3] = sin 108°/sin 36° = φ, [4] = 1. D² = 1 + φ² + φ² +
    1 = 2 + 2(φ + 1) = 4 + 2φ = 5 + √5 ≈ 7.236. (b) Empty link: n = 0, σ =
    0: Z = D⁻¹ = S₀₀. Unknot, framing 0: σ = 0, J(c) = d_c, Z = D⁻²∑d_c² =
    D⁻²D² = 1 = Z(S² × S¹). Framing +1: J(c) = θ_c d_c, σ = 1, ζ = p₊/D:
    Z = D⁻²(D/p₊)∑θ_c d_c² = D⁻²(D/p₊)p₊ = D⁻¹ ✓. Framing −1: σ = −1, Z =
    D⁻²(p₊/D)p₋ = D⁻¹ since p₊p₋ = D² ✓ (+1 and −1 surgery on the unknot
    are S³: the blow-up Kirby move). (c) k = 1: D² = 2, p₊ = 1 + i = √2
    e^{iπ/4}, ζ = e^{iπ/4} (c = 1). Z(L(p, 1)) = D⁻²ζ⁻¹∑θ_cᵖd_c² = ½
    e^{−iπ/4}(1 + iᵖ). p = 1: ½e^{−iπ/4}√2e^{iπ/4} = 1/√2 = Z(S³) ✓; p = 2:
    1 + i² = 0; p = 3: 1 − i = √2e^{−iπ/4}, Z = (√2/2)e^{−iπ/2} = −i/√2;
    p = 4: ½e^{−iπ/4}·2 = e^{−iπ/4}. |Z(RP³)| = 0 for this theory.
    Common mistakes: forgetting the framing factor ζ^{−σ} (without it the
    sum is not a 3-manifold invariant); using q = e^{2πi/(k+2)} instead of
    e^{πi/(k+2)} in [n]; normalising Z(S³) = 1 and then misusing Z(M # N).
    Now you try: for k = 2 compute d, D² and S₀₀; is S₁₁ zero?
    (1, √2, 1; 4; 1/2; yes, [4] = sin π/sin(π/4) = 0)

D11. (a) Derive the Verlinde formula dim V(Σ_g) = ∑ₓ S₀ₓ^{2−2g} from the
    gluing axioms and N_a = S·diag(S_{ax}/S_{0x})·S⁻¹. (b) Compute dim V(Σ_g)
    for SU(2)_1 and SU(2)_2 in closed form, and dim V(Σ₂) for SU(2)_k from
    theta-graph colourings for k = 1, 2, 3. (c) For the Fibonacci category
    (labels 1, τ; τ ⊗ τ = 1 ⊕ τ; S = (1/√(2 + φ))[[1, φ], [φ, −1]]) compute
    dim V(Σ_g) for g ≤ 4 and verify N_{ττ}^τ = 1 by the Verlinde formula.
    Answer: (b) 2^g; 2^{g−1}(2^g + 1); genus 2: 4, 10, 20 (= C(k+3, 3)).
    (c) 1, 2, 5, 15, 50.
    Teacher explains: (a) Cut Σ_g into g − 1 "handles" by a pants
    decomposition: V(Σ_g) = ⊕ over labels on cutting circles of products of
    fusion spaces. A handle attached along a circle labelled x contributes
    H = ∑_a N_a N_{a*} (a runs over labels on the handle's meridian), and
    closing up gives dim V(Σ_g) = Tr(H^{g−1}). The N_a commute and are
    simultaneously diagonalised by S with eigenvalues S_{ax}/S_{0x}
    (Verlinde); N_{a*} has the conjugate eigenvalues. So H has eigenvalues
    ∑_a |S_{ax}|²/S_{0x}² = 1/S_{0x}² by unitarity, and Tr H^{g−1} = ∑ₓ
    S_{0x}^{2−2g}. g = 1: number of labels; g = 0: ∑S₀ₓ² = 1. (b) SU(2)_1:
    S = (1/√2)[[1, 1], [1, −1]], S₀ₓ = 1/√2: 2·2^{g−1} = 2^g. SU(2)_2: S₀ₓ =
    ½, √2/2, ½: ∑ = 2·4^{g−1} + 2^{g−1} = 2^{g−1}(2^g + 1): 1, 3, 10, 36.
    Genus 2 = colourings (a, b, c) of the three edges of a theta graph with
    |a − b| ≤ c ≤ min(a + b, 2k − a − b), a + b + c even. k = 1: (0,0,0) and
    the 3 permutations of (0,1,1): 4. k = 2: (0,0,0), 3×(0,1,1), 3×(0,2,2),
    3×(1,1,2): 10. k = 3: add the new admissible triples with a label 3 or
    sum 6: 3×(0,3,3), 6×(1,2,3), (2,2,2) together with the k = 2 list: 10 +
    3 + 6 + 1 = 20 = C(6, 3). (c) S₀₁ = 1/√(2 + φ), S₀τ = φ/√(2 + φ):
    dim = (2 + φ)^{g−1}(1 + φ^{2−2g}). g = 2: (2 + φ)(1 + φ⁻²); using φ⁻² =
    2 − φ, this is (2 + φ)(3 − φ) = 6 + φ − φ² = 6 + φ − φ − 1 = 5. g = 3:
    (2 + φ)²(1 + φ⁻⁴) = 15, g = 4: 50 (numerically 13.090·1.146 = 15.00).
    N_{ττ}^τ = ∑ₓ S_{τx}³/S_{0x} = (φ³/1 + (−1)³/φ)/(2 + φ) = (φ³ − φ⁻¹)/(2 +
    φ) = (2φ + 1 − φ + 1)/(2 + φ) = 1 ✓ (φ³ = 2φ + 1, φ⁻¹ = φ − 1).
    Common mistakes: forgetting that S is symmetric unitary (so ∑_a |S_{ax}|²
    = 1); writing dim V(Σ_g) = (k + 1)^g (true only for g = 1, or k = 1).
    Now you try: dim V(Σ₃) for SU(2)_3 and the torus for Fibonacci. (120; 2)

D12. (a) Describe the Khovanov complex of a diagram and explain why its
    graded Euler characteristic is (q + q⁻¹)V(t)|_{t^{1/2} = −q}. (b) Compute
    Kh of the positive Hopf link from the cube of resolutions. (c) Given
    Kh(right trefoil) = q + q³ + t²q⁵ + t³q⁹ (over Q), find its chain group
    ranks, check the Euler characteristic, read off Rasmussen's s, and
    deduce the slice genus of T(2, 5) from s = 4.
    Answer: (b) 1 + q² + t²q⁴ + t²q⁶. (c) ranks 4, 6, 12, 8; χ_q = q + q³
    + q⁵ − q⁹ = (q + q⁻¹)(q² + q⁶ − q⁸); s = 2; g₄(T(2, 5)) = 2.
    Teacher explains: (a) For each of the 2ⁿ states s (0 = A-smoothing, 1 =
    B-smoothing, r = |s| = number of 1's) put V^{⊗k(s)}, k(s) = number of
    loops. Edges of the cube change one 0 to 1 and act by m (two loops
    merge) or Δ (one loop splits), with signs making squares anticommute;
    d² = 0. The complex C^r = ⊕_{|s|=r} V^{⊗k(s)}{r + n₊ − 2n₋}[shift −n₋].
    Euler characteristic: ∑_s (−1)^r q^r (q + q⁻¹)^{k(s)} (times the global
    shift) is the state sum of the bracket with A-terms ↦ 1, B-terms ↦ −q,
    d ↦ q + q⁻¹, i.e. the unnormalised Jones polynomial Ĵ. Invariance under
    Reidemeister moves is a chain-homotopy statement (Khovanov, Bar-Natan).
    (b) Hopf: n₊ = 2, n₋ = 0. Resolutions: 00: 2 loops, 01 and 10: 1 loop,
    11: 2 loops. Complex V⊗V → V ⊕ V → V⊗V, maps (m, m) then (Δ, −Δ).
    Degree 0: ker m on V⊗V is spanned by X⊗X and 1⊗X − X⊗1, q-degrees −2 and
    0, shifted by 0 + 2: q⁰ + q². Degree 1: ker(Δ, −Δ) = {(a, a)} has dim 2 =
    image of degree 0 (rank m = 2), so H¹ = 0. Degree 2: V⊗V/Δ(V), Δ(V) =
    span(1⊗X + X⊗1, X⊗X); the quotient has classes of 1⊗1 (deg 2) and 1⊗X
    (deg 0), shifted by 2 + 2: q⁶ + q⁴ in homological degree 2. Kh = 1 + q²
    + t²q⁴ + t²q⁶; χ = 1 + q² + q⁴ + q⁶ = (q + q⁻¹)(q + q⁵) and V(H₊) =
    −s − s⁵ at s = −q is q + q⁵ ✓. (c) Loops: r = 0: 2, r = 1: 1 (×3), r =
    2: 2 (×3), r = 3: 3: ranks 4, 3·2, 3·4, 8. χ at t = −1: q + q³ + q⁵ − q⁹;
    dividing by q + q⁻¹ gives q² + q⁶ − q⁸ = t + t³ − t⁴ at t = q² ✓. Lee's
    theory leaves two generators in homological degree 0 at q^{s−1}, q^{s+1}
    = q¹, q³: s = 2, and |s| ≤ 2g₄ gives g₄ ≥ 1 (the Seifert genus is 1).
    For T(2, 5): s = 4 ⇒ g₄ ≥ 2 ≥ g₄ (Seifert genus (2−1)(5−1)/2 = 2), so
    g₄(T(2, 5)) = 2: the Milnor conjecture for this knot.
    Common mistakes: forgetting the shifts (the answer then depends on the
    diagram); reading Kh at t = +1 instead of t = −1 for the Euler
    characteristic; thinking Kh is determined by V (5₁ and 10₁₃₂ share V
    up to mirroring but have different Kh).
    Now you try: Kh(left trefoil) and s(T(3, 4)).
    (q⁻¹ + q⁻³ + t⁻²q⁻⁵ + t⁻³q⁻⁹; s = (3 − 1)(4 − 1) = 6)

M6. R-matrices. Ř = [[q,0,0,0],[0,0,1,0],[0,1,q−q⁻¹,0],[0,0,0,q]], μ =

         (the left trefoil)

      j) yes; yes; yes
      k) q^{1/2} (×3), −q^{−3/2}
      l) 2 (ρ(σ₁) = Ř ⊗ 1 still satisfies the Hecke relation)


M7. Atiyah axioms: bookkeeping in a TQFT Z.
    a) Z(∅) as a vector space, and Z(∅) as a closed n-manifold
    b) 1D TQFT with Z(pt₊) = C⁵: Z(S¹), Z(S¹ ⊔ S¹), Z(pt₋)
    c) 2D TQFT with dim Z(S¹) = 4: Z(T²)
    d) 3D TQFT with dim Z(T²) = 7: Z(T³); which SU(2)_k has this?
    e) SU(2)_3: Z(Σ₂ × S¹)
    f) any 3D TQFT with Z(S²) = C: Z(S² × S¹)
    g) dims 3 and 4 for Z(Σ), Z(Σ′): dim Z(Σ ⊔ Σ′), dim Z(Σ̄)
    h) SU(2)_1: Z(S³ ⊔ S³)
    i) Z(M # N) if Z(M) = 2, Z(N) = 3, Z(S³) = 1/2
    j) check Z(S³ # S³) = Z(S³) with the connected-sum formula
    k) invertible 2D TQFT A = C, ε(1) = λ: Z(Σ_g) in terms of χ(Σ_g)
    l) the linear map Z(W) for W = Σ × I, and for W = Σ × I read as a
       cobordism Σ ⊔ Σ̄ → ∅
    m) number of pairs of pants and of cutting circles in a pants
       decomposition of Σ₂ and Σ₃
    n) Z(mapping torus of id_Σ) if dim Z(Σ) = 10
    o) can a TQFT have dim Z(Σ) = ∞?
    Answers:
      a) C; the number 1          b) 5; 25; (C⁵)* ≅ C⁵
      c) 4                        d) 7; k = 6 (dim V(T²) = k + 1)
      e) 20 = dim V(Σ₂)           f) 1
      g) 12; 3                    h) (1/√2)² = 1/2
      i) 2·3/(1/2) = 12           j) Z(S³)Z(S³)/Z(S³) = Z(S³) ✓
      k) λ^{1−g} = λ^{χ/2}
      l) id_{Z(Σ)}; the evaluation pairing Z(Σ) ⊗ Z(Σ)* → C
      m) 2 pants, 3 circles; 4 pants, 6 circles (2g − 2 and 3g − 3)
      n) Tr(id) = 10              o) no (zig-zag argument, D7)

M8. 2D TQFTs from commutative Frobenius algebras: give ω and Z(Σ_g) for
    g = 0, 1, 2, 3.
    a) A = C, ε(1) = 1           b) A = C, ε(1) = λ
    c) A = C², ε(e₁) = ε(e₂) = 1  d) A = C², ε = (1, 1/2)
    e) A = C³, ε = (1, 1, 1/2)    f) A = C², ε = (1/2, 1/2)
    g) C[x]/(x²), ε(x) = 1, ε(1) = 0
    h) C[x]/(x³), ε(x²) = 1, ε(1) = ε(x) = 0
    i) C[x]/(x² − 1), ε(x) = 1, ε(1) = 0
    j) C[x]/(x² − 1), ε(1) = 1, ε(x) = 0
    k) H*(S² × S²; C) = C[a, b]/(a², b²), ε(ab) = 1
    l) centre of C[Z/3], ε(g) = δ_{g,e}/3
    m) centre of C[S₃], ε(g) = δ_{g,e}/6
    n) centre of C[S₃], ε(g) = δ_{g,e}
    o) is C[x]/(x²) with ε(1) = 1, ε(x) = 0 a Frobenius algebra?
    Answers:
      a) ω = 1: 1, 1, 1, 1           b) ω = 1/λ: λ, 1, 1/λ, 1/λ²
      c) ω = 1: 2, 2, 2, 2           d) 3/2, 2, 3, 5 (1 + 2^{g−1})
      e) 5/2, 3, 4, 6 (2 + 2^{g−1})   f) ω = 2: 1, 2, 4, 8
      g) ω = 2x: 0, 2, 0, 0          h) ω = 3x²: 0, 3, 0, 0
      i) ω = 2x: 0, 2, 0, 8          j) ω = 2: 1, 2, 4, 8
      k) ω = 4ab (χ = 4): 0, 4, 0, 0
      l) 1/3, 3, 27, 243 (= 3^{2g−1})
      m) 1/6, 3, 81, 2673 (2·6^{2g−2} + 3^{2g−2})
      n) λ = 1/6, 1/6, 2/3: 1, 3, 27/2, 297/4
      o) no: x is orthogonal to everything (ε(x·1) = ε(x·x) = 0)

M9. Dijkgraaf-Witten theory (untwisted), Z(M) = |Hom(π₁M, G)|/|G|.

M10. Modular data, Reshetikhin-Turaev, Verlinde, Turaev-Viro.
